# CLASSIES CHRONICLES

## Season 00 — Foundation Phase
## Chapter 05 — The Signal Before Sunrise
## Episode 02 — Four Arrows, No Signature

The first mistake looked like a direction. The second looked like proof. By the time Enka understood the difference, eight people had handled the same message, four translations had repeated it, and no one could say who had first been entitled to give it.

---

## Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

Before sunrise, rain ticked against the louvers of the Koware Group’s New Legon Estates compound. Five single-storey, three-bedroom apartment blocks stood close enough that a light coming on in one block laid a square across the wall of the next. The shared boundaries made it easy to mistake proximity for agreement. Enka would think about that again before the morning ended.

At the research block, a photograph filled the display: a rectangular emergency notice, four language panels, and a bold directional arrow. Another image arrived. Then two more. Each had been photographed in a different part of a Kuala Lumpur building being observed by a remote Edubeam-associated team. The team’s assignment was to document anomalies and maintain contact, not to manage a building or direct its occupants.

“Four languages,” said the R&D director. “One arrow.”

“Four opportunities for the same error,” Enka answered.

At the far end of the remote observation channel, a team member paused under a corridor camera as a building staff member went past with a roll of temporary tape. No one was confronted; the team took a permitted photograph of the wall after the staff member passed. The first photograph looked ordinary. The second showed a differently positioned arrow. The third had a line under the arrow that the camera could scarcely resolve. In the fourth, a reflection ran across the text. None could settle what the building’s current authorized emergency procedure required.

An operations liaison recommended asking the building manager for the source instruction, its approval history and the current posted evacuation plan. At the conference table, Edliex listened in its customary silent phase. A narrow blue line on its console pulsed whenever someone spoke. The legal journalist entered the incident on the online pinboard, with a brief paper notice for the reception area: **UNVERIFIED INSTRUCTIONS — DO NOT TREAT PHOTOGRAPHS AS AUTHORITY**.

At the physical pinboard, someone stood on tiptoe to straighten the photograph. The stack of magnetic clips escaped its tray, marched down the metal frame and snapped onto the soles of his shoes. He took two ridiculous clanking steps before everyone noticed. It was the only moment the room allowed itself to laugh.

Edliex shifted into feedback mode. “Observed: four posted variants. Inferred: possible inconsistency. Not established: which, if any, is the controlled current instruction.” The team had learned to welcome the pauses between those categories.

Then conversational mode opened. Enka asked, “What action is justified?”

“Request an authoritative record. Preserve what was observed. Do not substitute our interpretation for the building’s responsible emergency personnel.”

Enka sent the field team a simple restriction: photograph and record from permitted positions; do not test either route or instruct occupants. If immediate danger arose, follow the responsible local emergency authorities and actual current safety arrangements, not the experimental team’s guesswork.

The team acknowledged. Behind the research block, Accra traffic began to growl awake. Across two cities and several screens, the arrows remained exactly where they had been. They had not become safer merely because people were now looking at them.

What troubled Enka was not that the notices disagreed. It was how confidently each one appeared to speak.

## Part 2: The Cropped Truth

The next morning, a single image of the notice reached Koware’s reception screen without the surrounding wall, the lower line or the panel beside it. Underneath, an impatient caption declared that investigators had already discovered which way people should go.

They had not.

The legal journalist removed the image from the public-facing draft of the pinboard and pinned the intact photograph beside it in the internal newsroom review. In the original, an indistinct line continued below the arrow. In the crop, that line vanished. The missing strip did not prove the cropped instruction false; it made the caption’s certainty indefensible.

Enka walked from the administration block to the media workroom, where the creative team was preparing a simple visual comparison. They placed a transparent border over the source photograph and showed the crop as a smaller rectangle inside it. An arrow was still visible in both. The surrounding context was not.

“People will remember the arrow before they remember our correction,” said the producer.

“Then make them remember the border,” the journalist replied.

Outside, a courier motorcycle passed the shared compound gate. At reception, a visitor asked whether the notice referred to a real public emergency. The concierge liaison checked the visitor register and gave a restrained answer: Koware was reviewing an unverified document in connection with an observation project; it was not issuing building instructions. No address or private operational detail was put on display.

The journalist clicked the spinning office chair backward to reach a cabinet, forgot that its wheels had unlocked, and glided away from the drawer in a slow, helpless retreat. He managed to keep both hands triumphantly on the untouched file as the room broke into laughter. Then he rolled back and opened it properly.

The day’s pinboard also carried a Ghana News Agency report on the government’s preference for media self-regulation. The newsroom placed that headline alongside its own question, not as evidence about the Kuala Lumpur notice but as a parallel civic lesson: editorial independence brings a duty to verify the whole context.

Edliex listened to the editorial debate, gave feedback only when invited, then answered Enka’s request for a minimal correction. “State the crop omits visible context. State there is no verified approved original. Do not manufacture a definitive route.”

The revised internal bulletin went to the team. The external post was withheld pending clearance and relevance. A colleague at a crowded Accra bus stop messaged the newsroom after seeing a forwarded screen grab. He wanted to know which route the picture proved was safe. The journalist answered only that no verified route determination had been made. The first viral caption had already been copied once; they could not make it unexist. They could, however, preserve the exact image and the exact words so the error would not become their own.

Late in the afternoon, a message came from the building manager: a distribution register might be in the records room. Enka enlarged the photograph one last time. The black border of the cropped image seemed almost too neat. Someone had removed precisely the portion that complicated the claim.

He wrote in the evidence notebook: **A truthful fragment is not necessarily a truthful instruction.**

## Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

The register arrived as a scanned attachment the following day. Its columns recorded distribution locations, copy counts and language versions. No approval signature appeared beside the originating instruction.

Enka laid the scan beside the four photographs. “This tells us copies were circulated,” he said.

The technical support and operations manager leaned over the table. “Not which source they were copied from. Not whether the source was current.”

“And not who authorized it,” Enka said.

The previous safety review had reminded them that recognizing a potential hazard and choosing controls were separate steps. The team was not qualified to improvise the building’s emergency plan from photographs. They divided the question without dividing the facts. The operations team preserved the files and their receipt times. The academic side drafted a controlled comparison procedure for the language panels, with independent linguistic verification still required. The legal journalist revised the pinboard to distinguish distribution from authorization. The field team remained observation-only and reported no attempt to follow either conflicting arrow.

At reception, the fourth printout lacked a clean space where an authorizing mark might have been expected. Nobody inferred that a mark had been deliberately erased. The manager’s email said only that the original instruction and approval record had not yet been located; the search would continue.

The concierge liaison prepared a tray for the arriving documents. A self-inking stamp, set down too close to the edge, bounced onto the floor, stamped a perfect **RECEIVED** on the side of a cardboard box, then rolled to a stop beneath a chair. It was a magnificently official declaration about the wrong object. The documents themselves were left unstamped until their provenance was recorded correctly.

A news item about Ghana’s recently launched automated road-enforcement system now occupied a corner of the pinboard. Staff debated the same distinction in a different setting: an electronic notice, however impressive, must still be authenticated through the proper channel. Nobody suggested the Ghanaian report explained the Kuala Lumpur incident.

Edliex’s feedback came in three short lines: “Distribution established by an unverified copy of a register. Approval not established. Translation fidelity not established.”

“Why say ‘unverified copy’?” asked Enka.

“Because the scan has not been independently authenticated,” Edliex replied in conversational mode. “A manager’s good-faith delivery is evidence of what was sent to us, not a substitute for the custodian’s original.”

The room went quiet. Their evidence was getting better and their right to act was not growing at the same speed.

Enka drafted a narrower records request: the source instruction, authorizer, effective date, controlled version history, language-approval records and any replacement notices. By evening the request had been sent to the building manager through the existing contact. Nothing had been posted for occupants, and no field action had changed.

Four sheets sat on the table like four witnesses who could all repeat the same sentence but none could swear who had first spoken it.

## Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

A new Millennium Probe reading lit the laboratory wall. The destination timestamp had changed again. The current date had not leapt forward with it; the displayed output belonged to a different clock altogether.

The R&D specialist opened the run history. Under a transparent cover lay an early Probe schematic in Enka’s handwriting. He recognized it immediately. The original design history and the cloned Edubeam’s first operation under the Micro Computer Technology Institute of London belonged beside the readings as archival story records—not as proof that any one output had physically relocated a person. Enka insisted that the raw inputs and previous readings remain preserved rather than replaced. The changing destination display, the historical reference and the present-day production clock would each retain its own label. A number with six decimal places was not, by itself, a travel order.

“Do we tell the Kuala Lumpur team the date changed?” the specialist asked.

“We can tell them what the console displayed. We cannot tell them that they changed century, building or physical position. Those are different claims.”

The proposed destination was a temporal calculation, not a verified scene that anyone could enter merely by naming it. The human team’s reported physical context remained Kuala Lumpur. Its exact individual positions, equipment condition and temporal relation to the Probe result were not confirmed in the material available to the control room.

Across the shared boundary, the academic team was examining a separate problem. The building manager had offered to circulate a clarification, but no authorized person had supplied approved wording. An employee suggested preparing a draft with a signature line ready for the manager. The quality colleague rejected the shortcut: a blank signature line could invite someone to treat an unapproved message as official.

During a tense review, the R&D specialist extended a telescopic pointer toward a graph. The pointer sprang shut with a metallic pop, leaving him presenting the audience with a handle no longer than a teaspoon. He stared at it for a beat. Even Enka smiled. Then the graph went back on the screen.

In the meeting, Edliex listened while the human team weighed clarity against authority. Its first feedback was unwelcome: “The control room cannot manufacture a building manager’s authorization. A draft written here cannot repair the approval chain there.”

“Suppose we write the safest possible instruction,” said one colleague.

“Safe under which verified current plan?” another asked.

That ended the argument. Instead, they prepared questions for the responsible building staff, including how a real emergency would be handled during verification. The staff member who had asked for a shortcut crossed it out personally.

By dusk, Enka had preserved the Probe response and the unsigned draft in different repositories with different status labels. The two puzzles shared a discipline but not necessarily a cause. The Probe calculation still needed its method; the notice still needed its lawful origin.

The night brought a fresh remote image. It did not show a signature. It showed a folded edge of paper, beneath which a thin line of smaller text could now be seen.

## Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

The morning comparison produced an almost embarrassing discovery: the four panels agreed more closely than anyone had expected. Their directions appeared consistent when translated back into a common working language. The original approved wording was still missing.

“A choir can sing the wrong song in perfect harmony,” the quality assurance manager said.

The academic registrar looked at the side-by-side printouts. “Agreement tells us something about copying. It does not tell us whether the first instruction should have been issued.”

With that distinction, the inquiry changed pace. A linguistically consistent set of copies could still reproduce an obsolete order. A clean distribution register could still omit authorization. The team set each fact in its own column, then listed the evidence required to promote it from working hypothesis to confirmed account.

At the facility block, someone opened a window to disperse a stubborn smell from the printer. The draft comparison sheets, left beneath the fan, rose together in a white flurry and landed on the quality manager’s head like an extravagant paper crown. The team retrieved them without altering their order. The quality manager took the crown off with solemn dignity and announced, “At least these copies agree on where to land.” It was the day’s one small mercy.

The legal journalist placed a verified report about transport unions suspending a planned Accra strike on the pinboard. He emphasized the difference between an announced action, its suspension, and the remaining demands. In the discussion that followed, a cleaner worried about what uncertainty in transport plans meant for staff getting home. The corporate manager worried about operational notices reaching people in time. Different jobs, different pressures, the same need to record what had actually changed.

Edliex withheld its conversational turn until the humans had exhausted their theories. Then it asked: “If all four copies match, what independent evidence tells us they match an authorized original?”

Nobody answered. That was the point.

Enka sent the building manager the comparison summary with the limitations on its first page. The remote team received only the observation policy and confirmation that building personnel remained responsible for any actual emergency response. No experimental reconnaissance would be converted into a route recommendation.

The smaller line in the photograph had now been enhanced enough to show that some text existed below the arrow. Its wording was still unreadable. An impatient colleague proposed filling it in from context. Enka refused. The absent words might change everything—or nothing. Guessing would change the evidence for certain.

The episode’s most convincing image had become four copies of a sentence nobody could yet authorize.

## Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

By the start of the next working week, the programme manager had made a board with two headings: **ACTION AUTHORIZED** and **ACTION REQUESTED**. The first column was short. The second had become crowded.

Under the damaged arrow in the source photograph, a tiny line of print finally resolved enough to invite transcription. It appeared to refer to a controlled issue or a condition on the sign’s use; neither the exact words nor their authority had been established. The team refused to publish a guessed transcription. Instead, they logged the region of the image as a separate evidence item, with the original, enhanced version, method and uncertainty preserved.

The production coordinator proposed a visit to a local sign-printing workshop to understand how such labels could be produced. It would not locate the Kuala Lumpur document’s source, but it could help them design a fair comparison exercise. Enka approved a limited, clearly educational visit, not a covert investigation. On a nearby Accra street the coordinator examined sample stock, edge trimming and print registrations while the liaison recorded openly discussed methods. No private customer records were requested.

Back at Koware, the programme manager unfurled the visit report. The telescopic display stand refused to lock; the whole chart slipped downward until the title **CONTROLLED COPY** hovered just above the floor. Its presenter had to finish one sentence in a crouch. The staff applauded the improvised delivery once, then returned to the technical material.

The legal journalist’s pinboard now linked two externally reported debates that mattered to staff for different reasons: transport safety and digital-record authentication. The cleaner noticed a photograph of faded road markings. The software colleague noticed the article’s description of electronic notices. The journalist asked each what question their job would send to management, and their answers were not the same.

Edliex listened, gave feedback, then entered conversational mode. “An enhanced image is a derived exhibit,” it said. “The original remains the reference. You may discover candidate characters in the pixels, but you have not discovered a signatory.”

The field team forwarded one more permitted photograph: the lower edge of the notice appeared to align with an older adhesive rectangle beneath it. That could indicate replacement, layering or routine re-posting. No one was close enough, authorized enough or safe enough to peel it back. The team kept its distance.

The programme manager imposed a deadline on the report, not on the truth. By the evening check-in, the group would document which requests were pending and who had authority to respond. A due date could organize human work; it could not force a missing signature into existence.

Before leaving, Enka found the earliest uncropped photograph again. In the margin someone had written a small annotation: **Ask who owned the instruction before asking who copied it.**

## Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The facility manager walked the compound at first light, checking entrances and shared boundaries before work gathered pace. Five buildings pressed against one another without losing their separate responsibilities. That was how he explained the notice problem when Enka met him by the gate.

“A message can cross a wall,” he said. “Authority does not cross automatically with it.”

The building manager in Kuala Lumpur had now confirmed that a records search was under way. The available correspondence still did not identify the person who approved the contested wording. Koware had the distribution scan, the photographs, the comparison, and a chain of receipt notes. It did not have the authorized master or a dependable link from master to posted copies.

The facility manager suggested mapping the evidence like a maintenance history. When a pump is serviced, a useful record shows the item, date, work, person and next inspection. A handwritten name alone is weak evidence; a system-generated mark alone is weak evidence; each needs a legitimate process behind it. The quality manager agreed to put the document equivalent into the case register.

A trolley wheel caught the seam between two paving stones as the manager carried an empty inspection clipboard. The clipboard launched into the air, flipped neatly and landed on his head like an officer’s peaked cap. He saluted with the pencil, recovered the book and continued walking. The shared courtyard had acquired its daily laugh.

Later, the legal journalist proposed a headline for the internal pinboard: **WHO SIGNED IT?** Enka changed it to **WHAT AUTHORIZING RECORD EXISTS?** The first question invited a face and a culprit before evidence. The second invited documents that could be tested.

They checked the external news desk too. A report about Ghana’s planned public-service digital transformation provided a reason for staff to discuss interoperability, audit trails and the limits of convenience. It had no known causal connection to the notice case. That distinction was printed beneath the headline so readers would not confuse thematic relevance with a lead.

Edliex’s feedback was unusually quiet: “One unknown may conceal several different failures. An instruction could be unapproved, approved but unrecoverable, obsolete, misfiled or incorrectly represented in the photographs. The record does not yet distinguish them.”

Enka asked the AI to turn the alternatives into a question list, not a verdict. The manager’s response would determine the next step. The field team stayed in observation status. No additional place, person or century was assigned to them by the Probe display.

As sunset changed the color of the compound wall, Enka scanned the record one more time. Every arrow was legible. Every copy had a place. The authority column remained blank because the evidence was blank, not because the team had forgotten to fill it.

For the first time he saw the blank as a useful result.

## Part 8: The Message That Stayed Unsent

The draft advisory occupied the centre of the control room screen. The legal journalist had written it in language that refused the temptation of drama: the reviewed photographs and distribution information did not establish the source, current validity or approval of the posted instruction; building occupants should seek direction from the responsible building authorities and applicable current emergency arrangements. The draft made no claim about which arrow should be followed.

It was clear, measured and potentially useful. It was also not Koware’s message to issue as an official building notice.

Enka read it aloud at the morning meeting. Edliex listened. The R&D director confirmed that no new Probe output had changed the field team’s verified physical status. The quality manager identified the remaining evidence gaps. The programme manager set out a communication chain that stopped at the building manager’s authority rather than running past it.

The journalist wanted to send the advisory to every contact on the building’s list. The facility manager objected: a broad message could be mistaken for an official emergency instruction. The journalist argued that silence could also be misunderstood. Neither dismissed the other’s risk.

Edliex entered feedback mode. “There are three actions, not one: preserve the draft; transmit a request for authoritative guidance to the responsible manager through the existing channel; publish or distribute only when the proper authority and content are confirmed.”

When conversational mode opened, Enka asked, “And the advisory itself?”

“Hold it.”

Someone at the edge of the table tried to pull out a jammed drawer of message receipts. It came loose suddenly, and a harmless rain of blank envelopes fluttered down around his shoulders. For one absurd second he looked like a magician who had failed to produce a single letter. The laughter passed quickly. No one tried the drawer again.

The team sent the narrow records request, not the broad advisory. The external-message queue showed the proposed advisory as **UNSENT — AWAITING AUTHORIZED CONTENT AND DISTRIBUTION**. The field observers received the same standing instruction: maintain lawful observation, do not test conflicting routes, and defer any real emergency to responsible local personnel. No fictional technological capability was promoted into a safety procedure.

A verified news clipping about Ghanaian law-enforcement training in data integrity remained pinned in reception. Beside it, the journalist left a smaller printed statement from their own internal case notes: *Evidence can be copied. Authority must be traced.* Both were marked with their proper scopes.

Just before the end-of-day review, an inbound attachment reached the case inbox. It contained a picture of a notice unlike the four already catalogued: the same bold arrow, a longer lower line, and a version mark too blurred to read. The sender’s identity and the image’s origin were not verified. The system assigned it an incoming evidence identifier; it did not treat it as a solution.

Enka asked the team to preserve the untouched attachment and its metadata, then to isolate it pending proper source validation. The quality manager opened an exception entry. Nobody altered the approved safety boundary. Nobody called the building to announce a discovery they had not made.

The message on the outgoing screen remained unsent.

Across the room, the newly received photograph displayed a detail the earlier photographs had concealed: below the arrow, there appeared to be room for two separate version marks.

Enka stared at the marks and understood how the next mistake could begin. If they called the image genuine because it was useful, they would be standing in front of the same danger that had started the case.

He closed the preview, kept the original, and said, “First we find out who sent it.”

The control-room clock continued forward. The historical record stayed where it belonged. The Millennium Probe quietly calculated a destination that no one in the room claimed to have reached.

And between the three clocks, the unanswered notice waited.

### A glimpse of the next episode

An apparent version mark, a missing approval trail and a fresh inbound image will force Koware’s researchers, journalists and facilities team to decide what evidence deserves trust before a new instruction is allowed to travel. The unanswered question is no longer only which notice is current; it is how an unverified message acquires the appearance of authority.

### THE CLASSIES CHALLENGE

Four versions of an emergency notice agree in translation, and a distribution register shows that copies were circulated. The approved original and authorization record have not been verified. What is the most defensible next step?

**A.** Treat the repeated translation as proof that the arrow is authoritative.  
**B.** Let the field team test both routes and report which feels safer.  
**C.** Preserve the evidence, request the current authorized source and guidance from responsible building personnel, and avoid issuing a competing instruction.  
**D.** Publish the cropped photograph because it is easier to understand.  
**E.** Sign the draft clarification internally so the investigation can move on.

## B4 — Mission and technology state

**Project arc:** Four-language emergency-notice provenance linked thematically to the Edubeam observation project. These are two *potentially distinct* investigations; this draft asserts **no proven causal connection** between notice anomalies and shifting Probe timestamps.

- **Edubeam mission ID, mode, actual roster and power/energy:** `UNKNOWN — not retrieved from approved mission state`. Do not imply a recharge, departure, rescue, intervention, travel event, device upgrade, injury or return.
- **Remote team standing rule:** observe from permitted positions, preserve records, avoid manipulating posted notices or testing disputed routes; defer real emergency instructions to current responsible local authorities. The user's earlier source draft documents observation-only handling; additional behavior here is proposed story continuity.
- **Koware control:** Enka, research and operations colleagues coordinate evidence handling in the Accra setting. Edliex is conversational in structured meetings, not the authority for building or organizational safety decisions.
- **Temporal logic:** shifting `probe_iso` can be an output of a dynamic calculation. No actual destination is determined from that value, and no live current source was acquired in this compilation run. Old fallback snapshots are not relabeled as fresh telemetry.
- **Incoming evidence:** fifth-like image and two blurred version-mark spaces are *only proposed Part 8 cliffhanger* and should not enter accepted mission state until editorially approved. Sender/asset provenance in-story remains unknown.
- **Primary risk:** staff and readers could misread a reused or cropped document as authorized public emergency guidance. The narrative's internal process is not an actual emergency route or a substitute for official plans.

| Arc | Start | End of eight-part draft | Disposition |

| Crop and media context | Cropped image circulated with overconfident caption | Internal correction made; wider spread outside team unknown | `ADVANCED / OPEN` |
| Translation comparison | Versions potentially divergent | Synthesis proposes apparent agreement; independent certified comparison absent | `PROPOSED / NOT CONFIRMED` |
| Small text/version trace | Lower line indistinct | Enhancement suggests issue/version context, but exact text unverified | `ADVANCED / OPEN` |
| Responsible building guidance | No authorized instruction supplied to Koware | Broad advisory remains unsent; responsible contacts asked for authorized records | `DEFERRED / OPEN` |
| Probe interpretation | Different outputs seen over successive runs | Source values preserved as observations; no spatial/temporal travel asserted | `ADVANCED / OPEN` |
| New inbound attachment | Not present | New untrusted image; origin unknown | `OPEN — NEW CLIFFHANGER` |
| Workplace interactions | Investigative pressure | Journalist and Enka acknowledge urgency-versus-authority tension; no rupture invented | `PROPOSED` |

**Eight slapstick beats:** P1 magnetic clips on shoes; P2 rolling chair; P3 stamp strikes box; P4 collapsing pointer; P5 paper crown; P6 sinking presentation stand; P7 falling clipboard; P8 falling blank envelopes. One discrete physical comedy incident per part, no additional comedic sequences required.

## B6 — Portable production handover: fifteen required elements

1. **Identity and revisions.** The working label is `S00_CH05_E02-`; current local narrative identifier `E02-8PART-DRAFT-20261114-01`, unaccepted. Eight supplied daily-folder names retain their spelling. The framework documents used as candidate references are CC-01.01/CC-02 suite v0.08.00. Approved version adoption and master editorial owner decision are not established.
2. **Calendar.** Provisional Ghana-local episode opens 2 November 2026 and ends 13 November; Parts 1–8 correspond to chapter working days 11–18. Two ordinary slots on 12–13 November have no supplied accepted chronicles. Owner-approved phase dates, timezone/calendar file and identifier migration remain unresolved.
3. **Temporal/spatial snapshot.** P: Accra scenes 2–11 November 2026; H: past source snippets reference July 2003 but no approved historical scene; F: one previous source excerpt quotes a changing Probe output in 2221. Koware HQ/Enka control Accra; team Kuala Lumpur per earlier draft; precise crew individual locations and Probe destination physical site unknown.
4. **What happened.** No acceptance record for the eight daily chronicles was provided. As a *proposed eight-part cut*, the team observes multilingual notice inconsistencies, critiques a crop, receives distribution information, isolates Probe ambiguity, compares copies, studies a lower line, cannot establish an authorizer, and holds a broad advisory while receiving an untrusted new image. Do not treat this as an accepted continuity summary.

Course completion and certified translations are not asserted. Date-appropriate study and H&S source integration remain a substantive editorial gap.

No weather claim beyond atmospheric scene texture is represented as measured.

Human owner/editor controls release and canon decisions.

Any continuation must load the **last accepted** episode state rather than assuming draft Part 8 was ratified. It may investigate the inbound image’s sender, authority trail and human stakes without claiming new route instructions.
15. **Operational truth.** The generated local Markdown public narrative and internal handover are separate download artifacts and one internal combined editorial review package. They were not saved to GitHub, signed, editorially accepted, released or illustrated. The public file ends at the single episode challenge; the combined editorial package is **internal only** because it appends the ledger.

### B8.3 Decisions proposed, not taken

- **Editorial option A:** approve an explicitly abridged eight-part episode, after source-fidelity and canon review, and keep Parts 9–10 as separate later unpublished chronicle positions; this requires a written exception to the ten-day ordinary compilation rule.
- **Editorial option B:** treat the eight-part cut as a **pre-final assembly**, recover and accept days 9–10 and compile a standard ten-part episode only then.
- **Recommended correction:** stable document identifiers should be enforced by a manifest independent of GitHub filenames, with a bidirectional alias table; otherwise CC-02 document numbering can misroute QA and ledger writes.
- **Recommended continuity strategy:** one event-sourced episode ledger owns accepted event truth; each character behavioral file stores a compact event pointer and a versioned materialized view, with access filtering and conflict-aware update control. Draft events never automatically update accepted canon.
- **Recommended upcoming derivative strategy:** treat this narrative revision as `SOURCE_UNAPPROVED`; do not use to claim canonical visual or video completion. When later authorized, associate every visual panel and video shot with an exact accepted scene anchor, a checked character image and rights state, an actual export filename and a real QA measurement.

# **Classies Chronicles — Episode Production**

The requested operation is an **episode compilation and narrative consolidation**, not another daily chronicle. The intended production sequence is:

1. Review the three Unified Production Master Prompt documents.
2. Review the four Daily Chronicle Generation Protocol documents.
3. Examine all eight supplied daily chronicles and their previous episode-production history.
4. Reconcile character appearances, relationships, organizational responsibilities, locations and unresolved storylines.
5. Produce one cohesive, action-packed episode, retaining the important events from all eight chronicles.
6. Append the Episode Continuity Ledger & Production Handover to the end of the episode.
7. Stop after the episode. Comic and media production remain separate, subsequent commissions.

I have also identified an important production constraint in the v0.08.00 master framework: an ordinary episode compilation expects ten individually accepted daily chronicles. Your supplied set contains eight. I will preserve that distinction rather than inventing two missing chronicles or misrepresenting the completeness of the source material.

The source review establishes the following important corrections, which have been incorporated into the revised manuscript:

- **Eight archive installments, ten working days.** E02.08 covers Wednesday through Friday. It remains one installment, not three invented chronicles.
- All eight individual chronicle source files are now identifiable in the current repository structure. This corrects earlier retrieval reports that E02.01 and E02.02 were unavailable.
- The previous platform-response file is not empty. It contains multiple competing episode drafts and recommendations. The consolidated version uses their compatible contributions without treating repeated AI-generated claims as independent evidence.
- All three governance charters, the governance reference and all 64 job-description files were retrieved. Their current draft status is respected.
- The organizational chart's role-day sequence has been reconciled with the character records.
- The five-block New Legon Estates headquarters, reception pinboard, quarterly governance arrangements and departmental representation rules have been incorporated.
- The story preserves exactly **one physical slapstick beat per archived daily chronicle**: eight in total.
- The final three-day installment retains the Legal Journalist's newsroom activity and the Edliex meeting sequence.

One important distinction: the v0.08.00 framework's strict ten-accepted-chronicle compilation requirement has not been met by the eight archive entries. Consequently, this is a complete provisional editorial synthesis, not an assertion that the episode has already achieved canonical acceptance.

# CLASSIES CHRONICLES

## Season 00 — Foundation Phase

### Chapter 05 — The Signal Before Sunrise

# Episode 02 — THE AUTHORITY BEHIND THE ARROW

**Working identifier:** `S00_CH05_E02`\
**Editorial revision:** `EP-ED-02`\
**Editorial status:** PROVISIONAL CONSOLIDATED MANUSCRIPT — AWAITING HUMAN REVIEW

# OUTPUT A — THE EPISODE

## Opening — Four directions, one unanswered question

Kuala Lumpur had not yet surrendered the night's rain.

Beneath the cold light of a lift lobby, four laminated notices caught four different reflections. They were written in Malay, English, Mandarin and Tamil.

Their headings made the same promise: instructions for moving safely in an emergency.

But their arrows disagreed.

Three indicated a stairwell to the left of the lift. The fourth indicated a corridor on the other side.

It hung slightly lower than the others. Its bottom edge carried a line of writing so fine that it disappeared in the first photograph.

The field analyst raised a camera.

Four photographs.

Four directions.

Four different versions of what appeared to be the same instruction.

The field lead checked the team's assignment. Observe. Record. Preserve. Obtain information through the appropriate channels.

They were not the building's emergency officers. They had no authority to test the routes, redirect occupants or declare any of the displayed instructions safe.

The analyst photographed the wall again, this time capturing the surrounding notice board, mounting positions and visible markings.

The fourth notice had something the others did not.

A date.

And beneath that date, a line nobody had yet managed to read.

Thousands of kilometres away, at the Koware Group's headquarters in New Legon Estates, Accra, Enka watched the photographs arrive.

Koware's headquarters occupied a close cluster of five single-storey, three-bedroom apartment blocks. Each block was managed by a department and shared a boundary with at least one other departmental block. Staff moved between the adjoining premises throughout the working day, while the reception area remained a common point of contact.

Enka studied the photographs on a large monitor.

Beside him, another screen displayed a saved historical reference associated with Las Vegas in 2003. A third showed the latest archived interpretation of the Millennium Probe's future-oriented calculations.

Three clocks.

Three different contexts.

None could tell him which emergency instruction the building in Kuala Lumpur was currently authorised to follow.

He enlarged the fourth photograph.

The lower line appeared again.

"Get the complete record," he said. "And find the person who was authorised to issue the instruction."

The investigation had begun.

## Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

The first meeting brought together Gifty Bright, Obed Ali and Angie Sylvia.

The Director of Research & Development had requested an examination of the photographs, with the R&D Lab Specialist and Liaison Technician responsible for preserving the evidence and maintaining the technical handover.

Gifty studied the images without speaking.

Obed placed the four photographs side by side.

Angie opened a record of the image sources, transmission details and available timestamps.

"Three arrows agree," Obed observed.

"That does not make them correct," Gifty replied.

"And the fourth could be newer," Angie added. "But we have no verified revision history."

Gifty nodded.

"Then we have four observations, not four votes."

She asked Obed to compare the mounting positions and physical characteristics of the notices. Angie was instructed to preserve the original image files separately from any cropped or enhanced versions.

Enka joined them.

The team laid four magnetic arrows on the table to illustrate the different directions.

One attached itself unexpectedly to the metal sleeve of a document tray. The other three followed it in a rattling little parade until all four were pointing up Enka's cuff.

He stood there momentarily, with the arrows apparently directing him towards the ceiling.

Obed removed them, one by one.

Nobody said anything until Angie finally placed the four magnets in separate evidence envelopes.

The brief laughter passed, and the investigation continued.

Gifty divided the case board into three columns:

**OBSERVED**

**REPORTED**

**AUTHORISED**

Under OBSERVED, they recorded the four notices and their different arrows.

Under REPORTED, they entered the building's location and the information received from the field team, distinguishing confirmed image details from unverified descriptions.

Under AUTHORISED, there was nothing.

That afternoon, Enka visited the reception area.

Michael McCauley, Koware's Legal Journalist, was updating the physical pinboard and its online counterpart.

A previously published Ghana News Agency announcement concerning the forthcoming 2027 Budget remained pinned. The announcement had identified 12 November as the presentation date.

The journalist had deliberately kept the card visible because the proposed New Economy programme could have implications for several departments.

Enka stopped before the board.

"Keep the original source and the publication date visible," he instructed. "And don't confuse an announcement with an approval."

Michael acknowledged the instruction.

Back in the R&D office, a new message arrived from Kuala Lumpur.

The field analyst had obtained an uncropped photograph.

The lower line was visible.

Its resolution, however, was insufficient to authenticate the wording.

The mystery had become sharper, not smaller.

## Part 2: The Cropped Truth

A photograph can tell the truth while hiding the fact that matters most.

Josephine Ohemeng understood that immediately when the latest image arrived on her screen.

The Human & Employment Resources Manager had convened Joycelyn Ennison, Esther Justine, Cornelius Jekora and other members of the support team to examine the communication and workplace implications of the developing inquiry.

Joycelyn, the Health & Safety Technician, requested the original photograph rather than the cropped version prepared for the management presentation.

"An image can be perfectly genuine and still be incomplete," she explained.

She opened the HS1030 training reference, *Proactive Safety: Mastering Hazard Identification & Risk Management*.

The principle was straightforward: identifying a potential hazard was not the same as having the authority or information required to determine the appropriate corrective action.

Josephine agreed.

The team needed to distinguish between documenting a concern, assessing its significance and authorising an intervention.

Meanwhile, Esther and Cornelius had a different concern.

The cleaning schedule, staff movement and daily service arrangements required coordination around the maintenance notices appearing on the reception board.

Pauline Mbuso, Maame Igbe Dwa and Vitus Quartey had their own operational responsibilities to consider.

The support team was not going to suspend its ordinary work because an international investigation had arrived on a computer screen.

A handwritten notice on a storage-room door had been taped too high.

Cornelius attempted to remove it while carrying several cleaning cloths. The loose sheet detached and landed squarely across the front of his overalls.

It read:

**KEEP THIS AREA CLEAR.**

He looked down at the cloths in his hands.

"That includes me, apparently."

Esther retrieved the notice and moved it to a more appropriate position.

Joycelyn used the incident to reinforce a practical point: a warning must be legible, appropriately positioned and consistent with the actual conditions it describes.

The Kuala Lumpur investigation was more serious.

A wrong emergency instruction could put lives at risk.

The field team requested three things from the responsible local building personnel:

1. The complete original notice.
2. Its creation and revision history.
3. The current approved emergency arrangements for the building.

They did not choose a stairwell.

They did not approach occupants to offer directions.

They did not attempt to reconstruct the missing text.

Later, the building manager acknowledged that notices were sometimes prepared by one administrative desk, translated by another and mounted by a third.

The manager could describe the usual process.

The manager could not yet produce the particular approved instruction that the displayed notices were supposed to reproduce.

Josephine entered the response into the record.

"Knowing how a process usually works," she said, "doesn't establish what happened on this occasion."

## Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

A file arrived from Malaysia with an official-looking cover.

It was a distribution register.

It listed the preparation and placement of the four notices. It provided a record of circulation, but it did not establish who approved the underlying emergency instruction.

Isaac Barry examined the document with Jacob Buckman, Byron O'Laughlin and Bonsu Bolt.

The Technical Support & Operations Manager asked for a preservation copy before anybody began editing the document for presentation.

Jacob recorded the correspondence and the questions requiring further clarification.

Byron considered the implications for the people who might eventually need accurate information.

Bonsu, the Concierge Liaison, raised a question that stopped the discussion.

"If a visitor sees four arrows, who is responsible for giving that visitor trustworthy guidance?"

"Qualified local building personnel," Isaac replied. "Not us sitting in Accra."

Isaac's team established a read-only comparison record so that subsequent edits to presentation documents could not silently replace the original evidence.

Bonsu was checking the printed register when the office printer began producing its pages in reverse order.

The final sheet slid off the tray and landed face down over his visitor badge.

For one moment, the badge appeared to identify him as:

**APPENDIX FOUR.**

Bonsu inspected it, removed the sheet and returned his badge to its proper position.

The laughter faded.

A further report from Kuala Lumpur indicated that a neighbouring notice board had recently been rearranged.

Nobody knew whether the fourth notice had been moved at the same time.

The field team photographed only what was visible and requested the appropriate records. They did not invent a witness or interrupt the building's occupants.

Isaac and Enka returned to the main case board.

Two headings were added:

**WHAT THE REGISTER SHOWS**

**WHAT THE REGISTER DOES NOT SHOW**

The number of copies belonged under the first.

The issuing authority, approval, translation accuracy and present safety implications belonged under the second.

The investigation now had a traceable distribution record.

It still had no verified approval.

Enka requested the source instruction, issuing office, approving officer, effective date, superseded versions and any recorded changes to the building's layout.

"An unsigned fourth notice might be wrong," he said. "It might also be the only one showing a later change. We cannot decide by looking at the wall."

## Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

The Millennium Probe moved again.

An earlier saved reading pointed to one future-oriented date. A subsequent recorded interpretation pointed to another.

The changing figures had attracted the attention of the academic department.

James Evans convened Steven Sarpong and Matthew Ebenezer to examine the implications for the research and educational records.

Steven wanted a single date for the presentation material.

James refused to choose one simply because it would make the document easier to print.

"The Probe has returned a reading," he explained. "That is not the same as confirming where a person is, where a team has travelled or what has happened to a mission."

They laid out the available information:

- Historical-reference timestamp.
- Production timestamp.
- Phase information.
- Amplitude and Probe values.
- Previous saved readings.
- Current source status.

Each reading retained its own acquisition context.

The differences could be investigated without claiming that a person had travelled, that a mission had changed destination or that a future event had become a historical fact.

Matthew arrived with a binder of discussion materials.

A loose label peeled from its cover, drifted through the air-conditioning and adhered neatly to James's sleeve.

It declared:

**FINAL REVISION.**

James looked at it.

"Then please don't revise me without asking."

Steven removed the label, and the meeting resumed.

Across the ocean, another attempt to identify the issuing office had reached a dead end.

Several administrative desks could acknowledge the distribution of the notices, but nobody could demonstrate the authority to approve the underlying instruction.

A manager's title, a distribution stamp and an official-looking letterhead were not substitutes for an approval trail.

Another field photograph arrived.

The faint line below the fourth arrow might refer to a revision or a condition, but the wording could not be validated.

Enka prohibited reconstructing the missing words from context.

James turned the matter into a source-literacy exercise.

How does one distinguish data from inference?

What happens when an instruction is translated?

Does a document retain its authority simply because the same words appear in several languages?

The continuing Septuagint study had already raised questions about the transmission and interpretation of words across languages.

The investigation was now presenting the same challenge in a practical setting.

"A machine can return a precise date," James said. "And a person can stamp a precise time. Neither proves the claim you wish the record would make."

By the end of the day, the fourth notice remained unresolved.

The paper had an arrow.

Nobody had established the authority behind it.

## Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

The academic department's next review brought together James Evans, Gloria Mica, Robert Amegashie and Moses Larbi.

Four translated versions were placed in parallel columns.

At first glance, they looked reassuringly similar.

Gloria examined the layout and the wording.

Robert reviewed the communication implications.

Moses, responsible for Quality Assurance & Compliance, asked the question that changed the direction of the meeting.

"Do we know whether these translations were independently prepared from an authorised master, or whether all four were copied from the same unverified draft?"

Nobody could answer.

James considered the possibilities.

The three matching arrows might represent a correct instruction.

They might equally represent three copies of the same mistake.

The fourth could be an error, a later amendment or a completely different instruction.

A ceiling fan lifted the unfastened corners of the four pages in sequence.

They bowed forward together like four delegates at a formal ceremony.

Moses caught them before they scattered, only to discover that the blank backing sheet had bowed along with them.

Gloria looked at the papers.

"Unanimous," she said, "including the page with nothing on it."

The brief laughter passed.

Moses returned to the review.

Agreement between copies was not proof of independent verification.

The team needed the original source, authenticated translations, revision history and confirmation of the building's current arrangements.

Meanwhile, Michael McCauley updated the reception pinboard.

One card was added:

**MULTILINGUAL SAFETY NOTICES — SOURCE AND APPROVAL UNCONFIRMED.**

The card was clearly marked as an internal information item. It was not presented as an emergency warning or an instruction to occupants.

Other public-news headlines remained alongside it.

The October budget announcement continued to attract attention from management.

The research staff were interested in developments in technology.

The support team wanted to understand the electricity maintenance schedule.

Different employees were looking at different parts of the board for different reasons.

Enka walked past it and stopped.

The problem was no longer simply that four arrows disagreed.

The problem was that the documents appeared more authoritative than the evidence behind them.

Late in the working session, a new lead arrived.

An older distribution work order might connect the printed notices to an archived transfer.

The number was:

**44-E.**

Enka asked the records liaison to pursue the document trail through the responsible offices.

No route test was authorised.

No public conclusion was issued.

The case had acquired a work-order number, but the original approval was still missing.

## Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

The weekend had not solved the case.

But the paper trail had acquired a second direction.

Work Order **44-E**.

Johnson Ortsin spread the document beside an updated site drawing.

Derek Shiraska examined the proposed activities and marked anything that could become an unauthorised route test.

Mathias Agbenorku brought a practical question.

"If the drawing is authentic, does that mean it is still suitable for the building as it stands today?"

Johnson had initially prepared a five-step plan that ended with a demonstration.

Derek drew a line through the final stage.

"We can rehearse the communication process on paper," he said. "We cannot rehearse an unfamiliar building's evacuation routes from this desk."

Johnson was visibly annoyed.

Derek was equally dissatisfied that the distinction had to be made.

Mathias listened to both sides before recommending that they revise the plan.

Johnson released the elastic band holding a rolled site drawing.

The roll sprang open across the table, flicked a harmless teaspoon into Mathias's empty cup and knocked the cup sideways with a terrific clatter.

All three men froze.

Enka inspected the empty cup.

"Fortunately, that plan contains no tea."

The room settled, and the investigation continued.

The work order established a possible installation history for the notices.

It did not establish that the person named in the building manager's account had approved the underlying instruction.

A records office had located a sealed storage box associated with an older transfer.

Its contents were unknown.

The custodian had not released it.

Meanwhile, Michael added another item to the online and physical pinboards.

The Electricity Company of Ghana had announced maintenance-related outages scheduled between 9 and 13 November in parts of Accra, Tema, Central and Volta.

The notice remained visible because the scheduled maintenance period was ongoing.

Mathias instructed his team to check the headquarters' own authorised continuity arrangements rather than assume that every named locality would experience an outage.

The specific Koware premises had not been confirmed as affected.

Back in Kuala Lumpur, the field analyst sent another image of the writing beneath the fourth arrow.

The text was still not sufficiently legible to validate.

The field team remained within its authorised observation scope.

Near the end of the day, a clerk's message arrived.

The box existed at the place indicated by the transfer record.

"We found it," Johnson said.

Derek looked at him.

"We found where it is."

Johnson paused.

Then he nodded.

For the first time since their disagreement began, he accepted the correction without argument.

## Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The release request arrived at the facility management desk.

Mubarak Mohsen examined it carefully.

Three elements were present:

- Transfer reference.
- Date.
- Administrative stamp.

The fourth was blank.

**AUTHORISED REQUESTER:**

No name.

Elijah Peter's arranged the outer envelopes without reading the correspondence inside.

Lovemore Saiga arrived with a tray of plant labels from the garden. Water had blurred the writing on several of them.

The dates remained visible.

The names had disappeared.

"Beautiful dates," Lovemore observed. "No idea what they belong to."

Elijah reached to move the tray.

Several slippery labels fanned out and one settled squarely on his shoulder.

It read:

**IDENTITY REQUIRED.**

Elijah stood momentarily at mock attention while Lovemore retrieved it.

Mubarak returned to the release request.

Enka had prepared a draft transmission for Kuala Lumpur.

Its opening sentence read:

*We have found the authorising instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Mubarak stopped him.

"We have located a sealed box that may contain a relevant file. We have not established its contents, and we do not have the authority to open it."

Enka read the sentence again.

Then he deleted it.

The field team's supplied location in Kuala Lumpur was not, by itself, proof of the identity, current position or condition of every deployed person.

A message sent to a plausible endpoint would not necessarily reach the right recipient.

The replacement message was narrower.

It requested:

1. The proper custodian's release procedure.
2. Confirmation of the person authorised to request inspection.
3. Authentication of the original issuing authority.
4. Verification of the instruction's effective date and revision.
5. Current guidance from competent local building personnel.

It contained no route instruction.

Mubarak marked the release form:

**FOR AUTHORITY REVIEW.**

His signature could confirm that he had reviewed an internal request. It could not make him the authorised custodian of Malaysian archive records.

Another question appeared on the screen:

**DOES THE ARCHIVED INSTRUCTION STILL MATCH THE BUILDING AS IT STANDS TODAY?**

Even the correct signature would not answer that question.

The box remained sealed.

The name remained missing.

And the fourth arrow still pointed in a direction nobody could authoritatively explain.

## Part 8: The Message That Stayed Unsent

The closing installment unfolded over three working days.

The first morning brought a fresh argument at the reception pinboard.

Josh Lafa and Terry Joan were reviewing a proposed internal headline about the Kuala Lumpur investigation.

The headline used one word that neither of them was prepared to defend.

**CONFIRMED.**

Michael McCauley crossed it out.

Beneath it, he wrote:

**REPORTED.**

The distinction mattered.

The distribution register showed that the notices had circulated. It did not show that the instruction had been approved.

Josh agreed to withdraw the stronger wording.

Terry revised the accompanying marketing note.

Neither wanted the organisation's communication to imply that it had verified something it had not.

"We can report the investigation," Josh said. "We cannot issue the building's emergency instruction."

The two departments worked together to revise the board.

The Legal Journalist retained the original headlines, source dates and publication references on both the physical and online pinboards.

A separate governance notice was also being prepared.

The next quarterly governance meeting would take place at a hired conference hall at a convenient location in Accra.

Its preparations included stakeholders, shareholders, the Board of Directors, member representatives, two representatives under the organisation's **God as CEO** arrangement and all top-level managers.

Video participation would be arranged for those unable to attend physically.

Leading top-level job roles were expected to attend.

The Legal Journalist was also preparing an internal note about departmental representation.

Within the IAPD Administrative department, the Corporate Secretary, Administrative Auditor and Legal Journalist would determine their departmental lead through their agreed quarterly or annual voting arrangement.

Michael had not assumed that the position belonged to him simply because he was preparing the governance brief.

The vote and its record would determine the representative.

The distinction between responsibility and authority was becoming difficult to ignore.

On the following day, Ghana's 2027 Budget was presented to Parliament.

The New Economy programme attracted considerable attention.

The government announced a US$10 billion investment programme covering 2027 to 2030, with GH¢30 billion earmarked for 2027.

Enka asked Michael to retain the original report and its date on the pinboard.

The headline represented a potential opportunity for the Group's research, enterprise and partnerships teams.

It did not represent an award of funding to Koware.

Pascal Quagrainie, Veronica Samiatu and NanaYaa Adom were reviewing international developments.

Malaysia appeared on their information board because of the ongoing Kuala Lumpur inquiry.

But the country was not one single story.

The emergency notices, international technology investigations and possible business partnerships required different sources, different authorities and different kinds of evidence.

Pascal instructed the team to keep the Malaysia AI-chip investigation separate from the emergency-notice inquiry.

There was no verified connection between them.

The team also discussed how international headlines could influence future Koware research and partnership opportunities.

The following morning, the reception pinboard received three more items.

The Ghana Stock Exchange was exploring blockchain and distributed-ledger technologies to modernise aspects of the capital market.

The Ecobank JoyBusiness SME Fair had opened at GHUD Park, Accra Mall, and was scheduled to run through the weekend.

And reports from Malaysia described an investigation into possible AI-chip re-exports.

The reports did not establish that an offence had occurred.

Michael pinned each story with its source and publication date.

The earlier budget announcement and ECG maintenance notice remained visible, their status updated rather than silently removed.

The newsroom had become a continuing record of events, not simply a collection of fresh headlines.

Meanwhile, Thomisia Daniel, Ingrid Razak and Ceda Bertto were considering the SME Fair.

Thomisia suggested an exploratory visit.

Ingrid wanted to hear directly from the participating businesses.

Ceda checked the practical arrangements and separated interest from a confirmed booking.

No delegation had been approved, and no expenditure had been authorised.

Ceda tried to push an overstuffed folder beneath the meeting-room door while carrying several loose attendance sheets.

The folder jammed.

The sheets sprang upwards.

One landed over Ceda's head like a paper helmet.

Across the top, in large letters, were the words:

**CONFIRMED ATTENDANCE.**

Thomisia removed it.

"A very clear label," she said, "on a thoroughly unconfirmed situation."

The laughter passed.

The Kuala Lumpur case returned to the main screen.

Enka placed the four photographs in a row.

The first three arrows agreed.

The fourth did not.

The date beneath the fourth arrow remained unverified.

Work Order 44-E demonstrated a possible installation history, not content approval.

The distribution register demonstrated circulation, not authority.

The transfer record identified the sealed box, not its contents or the authority to release them.

The manager's account, the register and the transfer slip were separate pieces of a history that nobody had yet reconstructed completely.

Edliex had been listening.

The AI had been active during the meeting, but it had not interrupted the initial reports.

Enka invited its feedback.

The status indicator changed.

"May I offer an observation?"

"Go ahead, Edliex."

"The same distinction appears in the budget report, the proposed fair visit, the chip investigation and the notice inquiry."

Enka waited.

"Receipt is not endorsement. A record of distribution is not proof of approval. Interest is not a booking. An allegation is not a finding."

The room remained quiet.

"Then give us a path through the paperwork," Enka said.

Edliex changed into conversational mode.

"I would suggest six verification stages."

It displayed them on the main screen.

**FIRST — ESTABLISH THE ORIGINAL SOURCE.**

Who created the instruction, and where is the authenticated original?

**SECOND — ESTABLISH THE APPROVING AUTHORITY.**

Who had the responsibility and authority to approve it?

**THIRD — VERIFY THE APPLICABLE REVISION.**

What was the effective date, and has the instruction been superseded?

**FOURTH — VERIFY PRESENT CONDITIONS.**

Does the archived instruction correspond with the building's current arrangements?

**FIFTH — VERIFY THE TRANSLATIONS.**

Do all four language versions communicate the same authorised instruction?

**SIXTH — VERIFY COMMUNICATION AND RECEIPT.**

Who received the instruction, and what evidence establishes that it reached its intended audience?

Derek considered the list.

"Would completing those stages allow us to announce which stairwell is safe?"

"No," Edliex replied. "The responsible, qualified local authority must make that determination. Our work can establish the limits of the evidence and identify the questions requiring resolution. It cannot replace the building's authorised safety process."

Mubarak nodded.

Enka turned back to the draft message.

A sentence had been prepared earlier:

*Use the direction shown on the three matching notices pending further advice.*

He read it aloud.

The sentence was short.

It was confident.

And it could be repeated by people who would never see the missing evidence.

"Delete it," he said.

The replacement requested permission to inspect the archive through an authenticated channel, confirmation of the issuing authority and current revision, and guidance from the competent local building personnel.

It instructed the field observers to preserve the evidence within their existing authorisation.

It supplied no route instruction.

A staff member moved towards the SEND button.

Mubarak pointed to the unresolved sender-authorisation field.

Isaac pointed to the field-channel authentication status.

Both remained incomplete.

Nobody pressed the button.

Enka moved the proposed message into the review queue.

The paper trail had advanced.

The case had not been solved.

The box remained sealed.

The fourth arrow remained unexplained.

The field team's last independently authenticated condition and the building's current emergency arrangements still required proper verification.

On the historical display, the past remained the past.

On the Millennium Probe, the future-oriented reading continued to change with its inputs.

Neither could erase the uncertainty of the present.

Outside, Accra moved into Friday afternoon.

Traffic pressed around the roads. Vendors called to customers. Employees returned to their respective departments.

Some were thinking about the budget.

Others were considering the SME Fair.

The research team was still examining the Probe.

The facility team was waiting for the proper release process.

And somewhere in Kuala Lumpur, four arrows continued to disagree on a wall.

Enka looked at the unsent message one final time.

The hardest action of the week had not been choosing a direction.

It had been refusing to invent one.

---

## A GLIMPSE OF THE NEXT EPISODE

### Episode 03 — The Custodian's Key

The next stage of the investigation may finally bring the team into contact with the person authorised to release the sealed archive.

But the identity of a custodian will not, by itself, establish the validity of the document inside.

The original instruction, its approval history and the building's current emergency arrangements must still be reconciled.

At New Legon, the SME Fair opportunity, the newly announced budget measures and the continuing international headlines will compete for attention.

The Millennium Probe may return another reading.

Edliex may have another question.

And the sealed box may finally reveal why the fourth notice was different.

Or it may introduce another mystery.

The box is still waiting.

## THE CLASSIES CHALLENGE — EPISODE 02

A building displays four translated emergency notices. Three arrows agree, but the fourth differs. A distribution register and a sealed archive box provide clues to the notices' history, but neither proves which instruction is current or safe.

**What evidence and authorisations must be established before the sealed archive can be released and anyone can treat one of the displayed routes as the building's operative emergency instruction?**

### Continuing characters

**Enka Yamoah — Corporate Manager**

Enka remains the central management character and investigation coordinator. His ordinary chart check days are 4 and 24, so his participation in this episode is a continuing narrative role rather than a claim that he was the daily check-day spotlight.

**Michael McCauley — Legal Journalist**

His chart check days are 4–5 and 24–25. His newsroom function continues across the episode.

He is responsible for factual reporting, source attribution and legal-policy research, not for providing regulated legal advice or approving corporate policy.

**Edliex — AI Desk**

Edliex participates in the meeting sequence under its established operating boundaries:

1. Listen.
2. Provide feedback when invited.
3. Enter conversational mode when activated.

It does not replace human approval or competent local safety authority.

## B3.2 Character development and relationship continuity

The following developments are proposed for editorial acceptance.

| Characters                   | Development                                            | Continuity implication                           |
| ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Enka and Gifty               | Enka accepts the need for evidence-led R&D conclusions | Greater confidence in Gifty's technical judgment |

These are scene-specific changes, not permanent personality or relationship declarations.

No romantic, family or private-history developments are introduced.

## B3.3 Character-memory architecture recommendation

The existing idea of copying continuity data into every featured character's file is useful, but it can become difficult to maintain as the cast and episode archive expand.

I recommend a small event-sourced character-memory system.

### Canonical structure

```
CC_AI_DESK/
└── 04_CANON/
    └── CHARACTERS/
        └── CHARACTER_NAME/
            ├── CHARACTER_NAME_character_cannon.md
            ├── CHARACTER_NAME_behavior_relationship_log.md
            └── CHARACTER_NAME_character_state_index.json
```

### Source of truth

The Episode Continuity Ledger remains the authoritative source for episode-level events.

Each accepted character event receives a unique identifier.

For example:

```
CC-S00-CH05-E02-P07-ENKA-MUBARAK-001
```

The character's individual behavior and relationship file should reference that event rather than reproduce the entire episode narrative.

### Required event fields

Every character event should contain:

- `event_id`
- `episode_id`
- `part_id`
- `story_date`
- `character_id`
- `other_character_ids`
- `event_type`
- `observed_action`
- `knowledge_change`
- `emotional_state`
- `relationship_delta`
- `duration_or_decay_condition`
- `source_reference`
- `acceptance_status`
- `supersedes_event_id`

### Acceptance workflow

1. Generate the episode and its continuity ledger.
2. Identify every character who actually participated.
3. Prepare proposed character-state events.
4. Submit the episode for editorial review.
5. Once accepted, append the approved event references to the relevant character files.
6. Rebuild the character's current-state index.
7. Flag affected comic and media references for continuity review.

Rejected events remain in the draft record but never enter accepted character memory.

Corrections should be appended as compensating events rather than silently overwriting historical entries.

This creates an auditable, scalable continuity system and avoids maintaining duplicate narrative histories across dozens of character files.

The field team may not:

- Direct occupants to a particular stairwell.
- Alter displayed notices.
- Open restricted archives without permission.
- Substitute a distribution register for an approved instruction.

# CLASSIES CHRONICLES

## Season 00 — Foundation Phase

### Chapter 05 — The Signal Before Sunrise

### Episode 02 — The Authority Behind the Arrow

**Episode identity:** S00_CH05_E02\
**Editorial status:** Provisional episode synthesis — awaiting human approval
