# OUTPUT A — THE CLASSIES CHRONICLES EPISODE

## Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

The first photographs arrived while the morning was still gathering over New Legon Estates.

The Koware Group's headquarters occupied five single-storey, three-bedroom apartment blocks clustered within the estate. Each block was managed by a different department, and the buildings shared boundary lines with neighbouring departmental blocks. Staff moved between them throughout the day, crossing the short paths and shared spaces that connected the compound.

It was a practical arrangement. A question raised in one department could reach another without waiting for a formal appointment.

Enka Yamoah was already at his desk when the first report reached him.

His morning study notes lay beside his research notebook. Eternal Dreamfall had entered Week 96 of its five-year Bible study, continuing the Septuagint reading through Leviticus and Numbers.

One passage had particularly interested him: Numbers 35:30.

The passage's emphasis on establishing a matter through proper testimony prompted a question that would remain with him throughout the investigation.

If several people repeated the same account, did that make the account independently verified?

Three people repeating one person's statement were still repeating one person's statement.

He wrote the thought in his notebook.

**A greater number of copies does not necessarily mean a greater number of independent witnesses.**

He had barely finished when Gifty Bright, Director of Research and Development, entered the laboratory discussion room.

Obed Ali, the R&D Lab Specialist, was already examining a communication received from the deployed team in Kuala Lumpur. Angie Sylvia, the Liaison Technician, had arranged the accompanying photographs and initial field observations.

The photographs showed four emergency notices displayed inside a building.

All four contained instructions in different language versions.

Three indicated a direction towards one stairwell.

The fourth indicated another direction.

The difference was immediately apparent.

The fourth notice also appeared to have been positioned slightly lower than the others. A printed date was visible near its lower edge.

Enka studied the photographs.

"Are we certain these are four independently prepared instructions?"

Angie shook her head.

"We know there are four displayed versions. We don't yet know how they were produced or whether they came from separate approved sources."

Obed enlarged the photographs.

"The direction is visible, but the issuing information isn't sufficiently clear. We should preserve the original images before anyone attempts to interpret the lower markings."

Gifty agreed.

The team prepared an evidence register containing:

- The location of each notice.
- Its displayed language.
- Its directional symbol.
- Its visible date.
- Its position relative to the other notices.
- The condition of the surrounding display area.
- The source and time of the photograph.

Enka was careful about the distinction between the three clocks being maintained by the wider Classies investigation.

The production clock recorded the present working date.

The historical reference clock concerned an earlier observation associated with Las Vegas.

The Millennium Probe maintained a separate future-oriented reading.

The field team's reported physical location remained Kuala Lumpur.

None of these records could be substituted for another.

A changed Probe reading did not establish that a person had travelled. A geographic position did not establish a temporal position. A historical observation did not establish what was happening in the present.

"Keep the records separate," Enka instructed. "We will not allow one unexplained reading to become an explanation for another."

He turned towards the magnetic display board to point out the four arrows.

A small magnetic arrow detached from the board and caught on the cuff of his jacket, pointing directly towards his wrist. For a moment, the investigation's principal directional evidence appeared to have selected its own investigator.

Gifty quietly removed it.

The laughter was brief, but the lesson was not lost on anyone.

The team returned to the photographs.

Nobody approached either stairwell to test the disputed directions.

The assignment remained observational. The team would document what was displayed, not decide which route occupants should use.

By the time the first report was completed, the investigation had acquired its central question:

**Who had the authority to issue the instruction behind the arrow?**

---

## Part 2: The Cropped Truth

The following morning brought another problem.

The latest photograph from Kuala Lumpur showed the fourth notice more clearly, but its lower portion had been cropped.

The missing section contained precisely the information the team needed.

The issuing office, a possible revision reference and a line of smaller text were no longer visible.

Enka examined the image twice.

"Why would anyone send us an incomplete photograph?"

Angie explained that the image had been extracted from a larger communication. It was possible that the crop had been made simply to enlarge the directional symbol.

That explanation was reasonable.

It was also unverified.

The Human & Employment Resources department was handling its scheduled organisational responsibilities. Josephine Ohemeng, the Human & Employment Resources Manager, was reviewing personnel and workplace documentation when Enka requested a discussion with Joycelyn Ennison, the Health & Safety Technician.

The cleaning and domestic support team was also involved in reviewing the practical implications of workplace notices.

Esther Justine, the Cleaning Technician, had been coordinating cleaning records with Pauline Mbuso, the Maid, while Cornelius Jekora, the Janitor, was attending to the common areas.

Maame Igbe Dwa, the Cooking Chef, and Vitus Quartey, the Liaison General, had their own operational responsibilities.

Joycelyn listened to the explanation before examining the photographs.

"An emergency notice is not simply an information sheet," she said. "People may act on it under pressure. If its instructions are wrong, incomplete or outdated, the consequences can be serious."

Josephine asked for the original photograph, its transmission history and the complete document from which it had been extracted.

Joycelyn then brought up the Health & Safety learning framework.

The relevant subject was:

**HS1000 — Fundamentals of Health & Safety**

**Topic 3 — Proactive Safety: Mastering Hazard Identification and Risk Management.**

The topic covered hazard identification, risk assessment, the hierarchy of controls and the maintenance of effective risk registers.

Joycelyn explained that the team needed to distinguish the hazard from the uncertainty surrounding the instruction.

The potential hazard was not simply that four notices disagreed.

It was that people might rely on an unverified direction during an emergency.

The appropriate response was to identify the risk, establish what was known, preserve the evidence and seek competent local verification.

She would not endorse a route merely because three notices agreed.

Josephine supported the approach.

She also pointed out that the administrative chain needed to be examined separately from the safety assessment.

Who had requested the notices?

Who had approved their preparation?

Who had authorized the translations?

Who had arranged their distribution?

And who had the responsibility to ensure that the notices remained appropriate for the building?

The questions were becoming more precise.

Later, Cornelius was moving his cleaning cart through the common passage when a loose notice label caught on the back of his apron. He continued with his work, unaware that the words IDENTIFICATION REQUIRED were travelling with him until Esther pointed it out.

The label was removed, and the team returned to the records.

By afternoon, the uncropped image had been requested through the appropriate communication channel.

The lower section appeared to contain a reference ending in **44-E**.

It was not a signature.

It was not an approval.

But it was the first identifiable administrative clue.

---

## Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

The next stage of the investigation moved from workplace documentation to technical records.

Isaac Barry, the Technical Support & Operations Manager, received the request through the appropriate service channel.

Bonsu Bolt, the Concierge Liaison, was assisting with the coordination of visitors, records requests and departmental communications.

The first priority was to preserve the information already received.

Isaac opened a technical incident record and separated the evidence into distinct categories:

1. Original photographs.
2. Cropped photographs.
3. Displayed notice versions.
4. Distribution information.
5. Communications concerning the notices.
6. Unverified claims about their approval.

He was particularly concerned that the original files should not be overwritten by improved or compressed versions.

Bonsu checked the available visitor and correspondence records to establish whether any representative had formally delivered the notices or associated documentation to the building.

The distinction mattered.

A record of someone delivering a document would not necessarily establish that the person had authority to approve its contents.

The distribution register eventually arrived.

It contained four entries corresponding to the four displayed versions.

The records showed that the notices had passed through a distribution process. There were references to preparation, printing and placement.

But the approval field was incomplete.

The fourth notice had no identifiable approving signature.

The team now had a more complicated problem.

The first three notices appeared to agree. Their distribution histories were similar. The fourth was different, but its difference did not automatically make it more reliable.

Isaac compared the available document references.

The four entries appeared to be connected to the same administrative process.

If the three matching versions had all been copied from one unapproved source, their agreement would prove very little.

Bonsu asked whether the original request had been signed by the person responsible for the building.

The answer was not yet available.

He therefore refused to mark the request as completed.

During the review, several loose copies were being transferred between the records tray and the visitor clipboard. A passing movement of air sent the papers across the desk, and one landed perfectly on Bonsu's clipboard.

He lifted it carefully, holding the loose sheet as though it were an official certificate.

Isaac removed it and returned it to the correct file.

The moment passed.

The records did not.

Enka reviewed the distribution register that afternoon.

"At least we now know that the notices were circulated," he said.

"Yes," Isaac replied. "But circulation and approval are different records."

Bonsu added that the same distinction applied to the visitor register.

Someone could have delivered the notices without being the person authorized to issue them.

Enka accepted the correction.

He instructed the team to preserve the distribution register and seek the original instruction.

The fourth notice remained unsigned.

The person behind it remained unidentified.

---

## Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

By Thursday, the investigation had reached a point where technical evidence and documentary authority were becoming difficult to separate.

Enka returned to the R&D laboratory.

The Millennium Probe display had changed again.

The future-oriented date differed from an earlier reading.

The laboratory specialist had preserved the previous record rather than replacing it with the latest result.

Enka examined the display.

"Has the system changed its destination?"

The specialist replied that a changed reading could not be interpreted without examining the underlying inputs and calculation history.

The Probe was not a conventional calendar.

Its output had to be understood in relation to the system's historical reference, current date, phase and other calculation parameters.

The distinction was important.

A displayed future date did not prove that the deployed team had travelled there.

Nor did the team's supplied physical location in Kuala Lumpur establish its temporal position.

Enka instructed the laboratory to preserve the raw readings, calculation inputs and previous observations independently.

The investigation then moved to another department.

James Evans, Director of Academic Programs, was reviewing academic documentation with Steven Sarpong, the Academic Registrar, and Matthew Ebenezer, the Academic Partnerships & Outreach Officer.

The three were discussing how institutions handled documents that passed through multiple stages of preparation, review and approval.

James was immediately interested in the four notices.

He considered the problem similar to an academic document that had been circulated before the final approval process had been completed.

A document might have been prepared correctly, reviewed by several people and distributed widely.

None of those activities necessarily established that it had been formally approved.

Steven explained that the academic records system required traceable corrections and identifiable approval history.

Matthew added that even a document prepared for an external partner could not be presented as an approved agreement simply because it had been exchanged between institutions.

The same principle applied to the notices.

James suggested that the team should organize the evidence into three separate columns:

**WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY SAYS**

**WHAT THE RECORDS SUGGEST**

**WHAT REMAINS UNVERIFIED**

Enka adopted the format.

The three columns also helped the team maintain a distinction between the notice investigation and the Millennium Probe.

One concerned an administrative instruction.

The other concerned the interpretation of a changing technical output.

Neither should be used to explain the other.

James reached for his course roadmap folio. A small adhesive revision label had caught on the edge of his patterned scarf, leaving the words FINAL REVISION visible across his chest until Steven removed it.

James returned to the discussion without interruption.

At the end of the day, the investigation had two principal unresolved questions:

Who authorized the emergency instruction?

And what evidence would establish that the displayed instructions still applied to the building?

The team had not yet found a person who could answer either question.

---

## Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

Friday's review brought together the Faculty Coordinator, Gloria Mica, the Quality Assurance & Compliance Manager, Moses Larbi, and Robert Amegashie, the Liaison Tutor.

Gloria placed the four notice versions in parallel columns.

Moses examined the wording, the revision references and the apparent similarities between the three matching versions.

He was not satisfied with their agreement.

"Have these translations been independently checked against an authorized original?"

Gloria examined the records again.

There was no evidence that they had.

The three matching versions could have been translated independently.

They could also have been copied from a common draft.

They might have been prepared at different times, using different source material.

The available records could not establish which explanation was correct.

Robert pointed out that a translation could accurately reproduce an incorrect instruction.

The translation process could be technically successful while the original message remained unreliable.

Moses agreed.

The quality review would therefore distinguish:

- Accuracy of the displayed translation.
- Accuracy of the original instruction.
- Approval of the original instruction.
- Approval of each translated version.
- Consistency between the displayed versions.
- Applicability to the building's present arrangements.

The team would not confuse those questions.

Enka returned to his Bible study notes.

The YouVersion Verse of the Day for that Friday was Philippians 4:8 (NIV).

Its emphasis on what is true, noble, right and worthy of attention seemed particularly relevant.

He considered the number of documents now accumulated on the investigation.

There were photographs, copies, registers, correspondence and a possible installation reference.

The amount of material was increasing.

But the amount of verified authority was not.

He wrote another sentence in his notebook:

**Truth must be established, not manufactured by repetition.**

Moses reviewed the four versions one more time.

The original source remained missing.

The approval record remained incomplete.

The current safety arrangements of the building had not been authenticated.

A desk fan disturbed the papers. The four notices lifted together, followed by the blank backing sheet, which bowed forward with the same solemn movement as the others.

Gloria watched the empty sheet join the procession.

"Unanimous," she observed, "including the page with nothing on it."

Moses caught the papers before they scattered.

The review continued.

By the end of the afternoon, the team had agreed that the four versions would be preserved without ranking any of them as the correct instruction.

The Legal Journalist updated the reception pinboard with a cautious internal information card:

**MULTILINGUAL SAFETY NOTICES — SOURCE AND APPROVAL UNCONFIRMED**

It was not an emergency instruction.

It was a statement of the investigation's status.

Meanwhile, the records liaison reported that an older distribution work order might contain further information about the installation process.

The reference was not yet available in full.

Its identifier, however, was becoming familiar.

**44-E.**

The investigation was moving from the notices themselves towards the records behind them.

---

## Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

The weekend passed without resolving the case.

On Monday, the investigation resumed with Johnson Ortsin, the Programme Manager, Derek Shiraska, the Production Coordinator, and Mathias Agbenorku, the Director of Operations.

Johnson placed the available work records beside an updated site drawing.

The most important reference was now visible.

**WORK ORDER 44-E.**

It appeared to document the installation or distribution of the notices.

Johnson initially believed this would allow the team to proceed with a practical demonstration.

Derek disagreed.

"A work order can establish that a task was carried out. It does not necessarily establish that the instruction being installed was approved."

Johnson looked at the proposed schedule.

The final stage involved a demonstration using the displayed directions.

Derek drew a line through it.

"We have not established that the directions are current. We cannot use an unverified instruction as the basis for a practical exercise."

Johnson was frustrated.

He had spent time preparing the schedule and did not want to restart the planning process.

Mathias, however, supported the correction.

The team could rehearse its documentation and communication process without testing an unfamiliar building's evacuation arrangements.

The distinction was important enough to justify changing the plan.

The work order established a possible installation history.

It did not establish:

- Who authorized the instruction.
- Whether the original wording was correct.
- Whether the translations were approved.
- Whether the building had changed.
- Whether the current emergency arrangements matched the displayed arrows.

Mathias then brought up another matter.

The Legal Journalist had circulated a planned electricity-maintenance schedule covering parts of Accra between 9 and 13 November.

The schedule included several communities, and Mathias wanted the operations team to review its own continuity arrangements.

He specifically instructed the team to verify whether the maintenance affected the New Legon Estates premises before declaring an outage.

A community name on a schedule was not sufficient evidence that the Koware compound would lose power.

The facilities and operations teams would therefore check the relevant supply information and their own approved backup-readiness procedures.

Meanwhile, the field analyst sent another photograph from Kuala Lumpur.

The lower line beneath the fourth arrow was visible, but its wording was still insufficiently clear to establish the issuing authority.

The records liaison also reported that the older transfer record pointed towards a storage box held by an archive custodian.

Johnson reached for the site plan.

As he released its elastic band, the rolled paper sprang open across the table, flicking a harmless teaspoon into Mathias's empty cup. The cup rattled across the table.

Everyone stopped.

Mathias lifted it and inspected the empty interior.

"Fortunately, the plan contains no tea."

The team restored the documents and continued.

By the end of the day, the records office had confirmed that the storage box existed at the location indicated by the transfer reference.

But the custodian had not released it.

Johnson looked at the latest message.

"We found it."

Derek corrected him.

"We found where it is."

Johnson accepted the distinction.

The next question was no longer simply where the original instruction might be.

It was who had the right to request its release.

---

## Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The following morning, Mubarak Mohsen, the Facility Manager, examined the archive release request.

Elijah Peters, the Facility Butler, had arranged the correspondence and supporting envelopes. Lovemore Saiga, the Landscape Gardener, had been attending to the compound's outdoor maintenance and had brought in a plant-label tray while the discussion was taking place.

Mubarak examined the form.

It contained a transfer reference.

It contained a date.

It carried an official-looking stamp.

But the most important field was blank.

**AUTHORIZED REQUESTER:**

No name had been entered.

Mubarak called Enka.

The draft message to the Kuala Lumpur team had already been prepared.

Its first sentence read:

*We have found the authorizing instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Mubarak stopped him.

"We have found a sealed box that may contain a relevant record. We have not found the instruction."

Enka looked at the screen.

He had allowed the discovery of the storage box to influence the wording of the message.

The correction was necessary.

He deleted the sentence.

The replacement request would state only what the team had established:

- The transfer reference had been located.
- A storage box associated with the earlier records existed.
- The box remained sealed.
- The custodian required a properly authorized requester.
- The issuing office and approval history remained unverified.

Mubarak also pointed out that the original instruction, even if recovered, might no longer reflect the building's present configuration.

The correct historical document would not automatically become the correct present-day instruction.

Elijah continued arranging the outer correspondence without opening restricted material.

Lovemore returned to his plant-label tray.

As Elijah reached to move it, one of the wet labels caught on his shoulder and settled there like an epaulette.

It read:

**IDENTITY REQUIRED.**

Mubarak removed it.

The team returned to the release request.

Enka accepted that the missing name was not a minor administrative omission.

It represented a missing authority.

Even a valid document could not be released to someone who lacked the right to request it.

The records liaison was instructed to establish the authorized requester through the appropriate corporate and custodial channels.

The team would not bypass the custodian.

The message to Kuala Lumpur remained under review.

There was another question on the form:

**DOES THE ORIGINAL INSTRUCTION STILL MATCH THE BUILDING AS IT STANDS TODAY?**

Even the correct signature would not answer that.

The archive box remained sealed.

The investigation had found the place where an answer might be stored.

It had not yet found the person entitled to open it.

---

## Part 8: The Message That Stayed Unsent

By Wednesday, the reception area had become one of the busiest places in the Koware compound.

The Legal Journalist, Michael McCauley, had updated both the physical reception pinboard and its online counterpart.

The board was not simply a collection of newspaper clippings.

It was a working information centre.

Each item carried its publication source, date, verification status and potential relevance to the Group.

The Kuala Lumpur investigation had its own clearly identified section.

Josh Lafa, the Public Relations & Press Coordinator, was reviewing a proposed internal communication when Michael noticed an unsupported word.

**CONFIRMED.**

Michael crossed it out.

Below it, he wrote:

**REPORTED.**

Josh examined the correction.

"The work order indicates that the notices were distributed."

"Yes," Michael replied. "But we still have no authenticated evidence that the instruction was approved."

Terry Joan, the Marketing Manager, agreed that the distinction needed to be preserved.

A dramatic headline could attract attention, but it could also turn an unresolved investigation into a misleading public claim.

The proposed headline was withdrawn.

The pinboard retained the investigation under:

**AUTHORITY NOT YET VERIFIED.**

Other news occupied the remaining space.

A forthcoming Ghanaian budget presentation had attracted attention. The Finance Minister was scheduled to present the 2027 Budget to Parliament on Thursday, 12 November.

The Legal Journalist retained the announcement as a forthcoming event rather than reporting its contents before the presentation.

The planned ECG maintenance schedule for 9–13 November also remained on the board. Mathias had asked the operations team to verify any specific effect on Koware rather than assume that the entire New Legon compound would be affected.

The different departments were following different stories.

The R&D team was interested in Ghana's developing technology and energy priorities.

The Human & Employment Resources team was concerned with workplace resilience and employee readiness.

The Corporate Manager was watching developments that might affect future business opportunities.

The Events & Tours team was monitoring upcoming business exhibitions and community activities.

The International Relations team was following developments outside Ghana.

There was no requirement for every employee to be interested in the same headline.

On Thursday, the 2027 Budget was presented to Parliament.

The Legal Journalist updated the board with the actual announcement.

The proposed New Economy programme was expected to involve a US$10 billion investment over 2027–2030, with GH¢30 billion allocated for 2027.

The announcement also included a proposed nuclear-readiness programme, including plans to train more than 2,000 scientists and build local technical capacity.

Gifty Bright's department would have a legitimate reason to examine the research and skills implications.

But an announced government programme was not a Koware contract.

No department was authorized to claim funding, partnership or participation merely because the headline appeared on the board.

Another international development demanded attention.

Microsoft experienced a widespread disruption affecting services including Outlook, Teams and Copilot.

The outage interrupted ordinary digital communications and raised questions about the dependence of organizations on shared technology infrastructure.

At Koware, the incident became an immediate continuity discussion.

The team reviewed its ability to preserve records and maintain essential communications when a cloud service was unavailable.

Paper registers, locally retained records and approved alternative contact channels were considered as continuity measures.

The Microsoft incident did not explain the Kuala Lumpur notice problem, but it reinforced a lesson already emerging from the investigation.

An organization needed more than one way to establish and communicate what was happening.

By Friday, the reception board had acquired several additional headlines.

The Ecobank JoyBusiness SME Fair had opened at GHUD Park, Accra Mall, and was scheduled to run through the weekend.

Thomisia Daniel, the Events & Tours Coordinator, suggested an observational visit to assess the participating businesses and identify possible outreach opportunities.

Ingrid Razak, the Community Outreach Officer, wanted to understand what local exhibitors needed.

Ceda Bertto, the Booking Agent, separated expressions of interest from actual bookings.

No delegation had yet been approved.

No transport had been booked.

No expenditure had been authorized.

Another Ghanaian business headline concerned Telecel Cash's introduction of a network-agnostic e-wallet and Telecel Shop within the Telecel Play App.

The Marketing team considered the development relevant to the wider digital-commerce environment.

The Ghana Stock Exchange was also reported to be exploring blockchain and distributed-ledger technology for the capital market.

The International Relations desk had a different concern.

Reporting from Malaysia described an investigation into whether AI chips imported by a local unit of a cloud-computing company had subsequently been re-exported.

Michael made sure that the report was accurately described as an investigation.

It did not establish that an offence had occurred.

Pascal Quagrainie, the International Relations Coordinator, reviewed the report with Veronica Samiatu, the Global Partnerships Registrar, and NanaYaa Adom, the Global Partnerships Liaison.

They agreed that the Malaysian technology investigation and the Kuala Lumpur emergency-notice inquiry were separate matters.

The fact that both concerned Malaysia did not make them part of the same case.

The distinction was entered into the research register.

Meanwhile, Ceda attempted to push an overstuffed folder beneath the meeting-room door while carrying loose attendance sheets. The folder jammed, the sheets sprang upwards, and one settled over Ceda's head like a paper helmet.

The words CONFIRMED ATTENDANCE appeared above someone who could no longer see the room.

Thomisia removed it.

"A very clear label," she said, "on a thoroughly unconfirmed situation."

It was the only slapstick interruption in the closing three-day installment.

The laughter subsided as the Kuala Lumpur investigation returned to the main meeting screen.

Enka had arranged the four photographs in a row.

Three arrows pointed in one direction.

The fourth pointed elsewhere.

Work Order 44-E provided a possible installation history.

The distribution register established that the notices had been circulated.

The sealed archive box might contain an earlier administrative record.

But no authenticated approval had been recovered.

The building manager's current account had not established that the manager had issued the original instruction.

The current emergency arrangements remained unverified.

Enka looked towards the Edliex interface.

"Alex, give us your assessment."

Edliex had been listening throughout the initial portion of the meeting.

Its status light changed.

"Before offering an assessment, I have separated the evidence into four categories," it began.

"First, the displayed notices and their photographed contents.

"Second, the administrative records associated with their preparation and distribution.

"Third, the unresolved authority and revision history.

"Fourth, the current physical arrangements of the building, which have not been independently established by this team."

Enka listened.

"Your recommendation?"

"Do not treat the majority of displayed arrows as proof of correctness. Three agreeing documents may share one unverified source. The fourth may be incorrect, but its difference does not establish that it is correct either."

Moses agreed.

Edliex continued.

"The same distinction applies to the other information presented today. A government announcement is not a Koware funding award. An expression of interest is not a confirmed booking. An investigation is not a finding of guilt. A record of distribution is not an approval record."

The light shifted again.

Edliex entered conversational mode.

Enka leaned towards the screen.

"Then give us a way to move the investigation forward."

Edliex proposed a six-stage verification process.

**Stage One — Establish the original source.**

Identify the document from which the four notices were prepared.

**Stage Two — Establish the issuing and approving authority.**

Determine who requested, prepared, reviewed and authorized the original instruction.

**Stage Three — Establish the applicable revision.**

Verify its date, version, distribution history and any later amendments.

**Stage Four — Verify the present physical arrangements.**

Obtain confirmation from the qualified local personnel responsible for the building's current safety arrangements.

**Stage Five — Verify the translations.**

Establish whether each displayed version faithfully represents the authorized original.

**Stage Six — Verify communication and receipt.**

Determine which versions were distributed, who received them and whether the intended recipients were given the correct information.

Derek examined the proposed process.

"Would that allow our team to announce which stairwell is safe?"

"No," Edliex replied. "The team does not possess the authority or verified site information required to make that determination. The competent local building authority must establish the operative emergency arrangements."

Enka considered the response.

The six stages were not a substitute for the building's safety procedures.

They were a method for finding the evidence and authority needed to resolve the documentary investigation.

He opened the draft communication.

The first version still contained the sentence:

*We have found the authorizing instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

He read it again.

It was wrong.

They had found a possible storage location, not the instruction.

They had found a distribution record, not an approval.

They had not authenticated the sender's authority or the field communication channel.

Enka deleted the sentence.

The replacement message requested:

- Confirmation of the issuing office.
- Identification of the authorized requester.
- Permission for the records custodian to release the relevant archive material.
- Verification of the applicable revision.
- Confirmation of the building's present emergency arrangements by qualified local personnel.
- Preservation of the four photographed notices and their distribution records.

It did not contain an evacuation direction.

It did not authorize an inspection.

It did not instruct anyone to use one stairwell instead of another.

The draft was ready for review.

Mubarak examined the sender-authorization field.

It was incomplete.

Isaac checked the communication channel.

Authentication had not been established.

The room became quiet.

The staff member closest to the screen moved towards the SEND control.

Enka raised his hand.

"Not yet."

No one pressed the button.

The message remained in the review queue.

The case had advanced considerably since the first photograph arrived.

The team now had four notices, a distribution register, Work Order 44-E, a possible archive location, a defined verification process and an increasingly clear understanding of what remained unknown.

But the original instruction was still missing.

The approval authority was still unidentified.

The current safety arrangements were still unverified.

Outside the compound, Accra continued with its ordinary Friday activity. The headlines on the reception board remained visible to employees and visitors. Some would be replaced as the news changed. Others would stay because their consequences were still developing.

The Malaysia investigation would continue as a separate international story.

The SME Fair could become an opportunity for the Events & Tours team if the appropriate arrangements were approved.

The new digital-commerce developments would receive further attention.

The Millennium Probe would retain its own technical record.

And the Kuala Lumpur notices would remain unresolved.

Enka looked at the unsent message.

The hardest decision of the week had not been choosing a direction.

It had been refusing to invent one.

---

## PREVIEW — EPISODE 03: THE CUSTODIAN'S KEY

The next stage begins with the person who controls the sealed archive.

A properly authorized requester may finally emerge, but the contents of the box are still unknown.

At New Legon, the new budget announcements, digital-commerce developments and proposed SME Fair visit will create competing priorities. The International Relations team will continue separating genuine international opportunities from unverified claims.

Edliex has established a method for tracing the missing instruction.

The remaining question is whether the people holding the records can establish the authority to release them.

The box is still sealed.

## THE CLASSIES CHALLENGE — EPISODE 02

Four translated emergency notices are displayed inside a building. Three contain matching directional arrows, while the fourth points elsewhere.

A distribution register and a sealed archive box provide clues about their history, but neither establishes which instruction is currently approved or safe.

**What independent evidence, authorizations and present-day safety verification must be obtained before anyone can treat one of those notices as the building's operative emergency instruction?**

---

# CLASSIES CHRONICLES  
## Season 00 — Foundation Phase · Chapter 05 — *The Signal Before Sunrise*  
### Episode 02 — *The Notice in Four Languages*  
**Provisional episode synthesis · 2–13 November 2026**  
**Editorial draft — not approved for publication**

**Production note:** This is a story synthesis of the eight supplied installments, not a claim that ten individually accepted daily chronicles exist. The supplied E02.08 draft combines the final three working days, 11–13 November, into one closing installment. The episode and day numbering, calendar, and legacy archive mapping remain provisional. Nothing has been committed or published.

## The Notice in Four Languages

### Monday, 2 November — Four copies

Before sunrise in New Legon, Enka returned to his notes from the Eternal Dreamfall study. The week’s working theme was the Septuagint and the long journey of meaning across languages. The plan in hand did not establish a passage for that particular day, so he left the daily reading line blank.

On the table, four photographs waited.

The notices were in four languages. Three appeared to direct building occupants toward one stairwell; the fourth pointed elsewhere. Their layout looked official. The field team’s report, relayed from Kuala Lumpur in this fictional investigation, was observational: the notices had been recorded, but no one had chosen a route or tested one.

“Four languages,” Enka said, “but how many sources?”

The R&D Lab Specialist did not guess. “We don’t know yet. They may all come from one original.”

Enka wrote **OBSERVED** at the top of a page and listed only what the photographs showed: four versions, several languages, inconsistent directions, and a date visible on one copy. Beneath it he wrote **NOT YET VERIFIED**. The original instruction, its author, its approval, and the relationship between the translations all belonged there.

The date on the odd notice drew their eyes. It might help trace the document. It did not make the notice right—or wrong.

The distinction felt familiar to Enka. A copy could carry a message far from its source and still fail to show who had authorized it. For now, the team would preserve the evidence and keep the investigation open.

### Tuesday, 3 November — The person behind the notice

The next day, Enka’s questions narrowed. Who had requested the notice? Who had prepared its original wording? Who approved the translations, and when were the different versions displayed?

The building manager was asked to check the records. The request was deliberate: ask for the chain of responsibility, not for someone to endorse whichever copy looked most convincing.

At the Koware briefing, the chapter’s working-day role check placed Health & Safety and personnel-support responsibilities in view. Enka treated that as a reason to consider how a public instruction might affect people—not as proof that any named employee had performed a real task.

The uncertainty was not a license to improvise. The assignment remained observational, and the team had no authority to decide which route people should use. Enka asked the lab to keep the photographs and questions together, so that a later document would not be mistaken for an answer before anyone checked what it could actually establish.

The original authorization was still missing. But the inquiry now had a person to ask and a record trail to seek.

### Wednesday, 4 November — A record, not an answer

A response arrived with a distribution register. It showed that copies had been issued. It did not identify who had approved the source instruction.

Enka placed the register beside the four photographs.

“What does it prove?” he asked.

“That the copies were tracked,” the specialist replied. “Not that the instruction was authorized.”

The team added the register to the **OBSERVED** column and left the approval question open. A record’s silence about a signature could not prove that no signature had ever existed; it showed only that this record did not supply one.

Several draft accounts of this day in the archive disagree about other events. The episode does not treat any of those disputed developments as settled. The durable thread is the notice investigation: circulation is not authorization, and the source remains unknown.

A date-specific verse, a specific Health & Safety lesson, and a fully verified headline set were not established in the material used for this episode. Enka did not fill those gaps with invented certainty.

### Thursday, 5 November — The date beside the record

The Millennium Probe display showed a different calculated date from earlier records. The exact reading and the explanation for its variation were not consistent across the drafts. Enka told the lab to preserve each source and its timestamp rather than overwrite one reading with another.

“The date can inform a question,” he said. “It cannot answer a different one.”

The field team’s supplied geographic position was Kuala Lumpur. A Probe coordinate did not prove where the team was in time, that anyone had travelled, or that a mission state had changed. The investigation into the notices remained separate from the instrument’s changing output.

The work-order trail supplied another useful distinction. A record could indicate that something had been distributed without establishing who had the authority to approve it. The team prepared a more precise request: find the original instruction, its approval record, its effective date, and the authorized versions.

No one in Koware’s fictional inquiry claimed authority over the facility’s current emergency arrangements. If there was an immediate safety concern, that belonged with the responsible local people and their verified procedures—not with a remote team counting copies.

### Friday, 6 November — The copies that agreed

By Friday, the four versions were lined up again. Three appeared to agree. The fourth differed.

The Director of Academic Programs studied them with the Faculty Coordinator and the Quality Assurance & Compliance Manager. Their roles gave the conversation different angles: how clearly an instruction had been communicated, how it might be understood, and how to document a discrepancy without disguising it as a finding.

“Three agreeing copies aren’t necessarily three independent witnesses,” the Faculty Coordinator said. “They could all descend from the same mistake.”

The quality-assurance lead treated the conflicting instruction as a potential hazard requiring proper assessment, not a puzzle for majority vote. The Director asked the group to preserve every version and explain the uncertainty plainly.

A real safety decision could not be made from appearance, repetition, or a changing Probe display. The source, revision history, applicable plan, and responsible authority still mattered.

The three copies that agreed had not solved the mystery. They had made the central question sharper: **what, exactly, did their agreement depend on?**

### Monday, 9 November — The line beneath the arrow

When the team revisited the notices, a possible source trail had emerged: Work Order 44-E and a reference to a records transfer. The documents suggested that a box might exist. They did not establish what it contained or who could authorize its release.

The Programme Manager had begun with a plan to rehearse the competing routes. The Production Coordinator stopped the plan before it became a physical test. They drew two columns instead: **WHAT WE CAN REVIEW** and **WHAT REQUIRES AUTHORITY**.

The Director of Operations helped redirect the work toward preserving the copies, identifying the competent local safety authority, and requesting the current site plan. Any rehearsal would remain on paper until a verified procedure and appropriate authorization existed.

At the reception pinboard, the Legal Journalist kept headlines and claims in their proper categories. A report, a forecast, and an official finding were not interchangeable. The team’s own uncertainty deserved the same care.

By the end of the day, the location of a possible records box had become a lead—not a discovery of the original instruction. When a custodian required a documented request from the responsible office, no one tried to bypass that boundary.

The arrow on the page still pointed somewhere. The investigation still had to establish who had the right to draw it.

### Tuesday, 10 November — The name the paper could not give

The transfer paperwork looked complete until the team compared what each line could prove. It showed a date and a reference, but not a verified authorizing name.

A proposed message to the field team said, *We have found the instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Enka read it, then removed the first sentence. The instruction had not been found. A possible container had.

The revised draft asked for a properly authorized review, preservation of the conflicting copies, and guidance from the responsible local safety authority. It remained a request for verification—not an operational command—and was not treated as delivered.

A real-world Accra maintenance notice on the reception board also illustrated why dates and locations must be read carefully: a notice for one community or day cannot automatically be applied to another. At the lab, the separate Probe coordinate remained a reference, not a confirmed team position.

Two folders lay on the table: one documenting the transfer trail, the other holding an unfinished request for permission. The archive box stayed sealed.

### Wednesday, 11 November — The empty space

At the pinboard, the Legal Journalist crossed out **CONFIRMED** and wrote **REPORTED**.

The change was small enough to miss if someone hurried past. It mattered. The Kuala Lumpur inquiry had a distribution record, but the approving authority was still unknown. The Public Relations & Press Coordinator withheld an unsupported conclusion. The Marketing Manager withdrew a headline that made the unresolved inquiry sound settled.

On the board, the Legal Journalist reserved a blank space:

**AUTHORITY NOT YET VERIFIED**

The blank was more honest than a confident caption.

### Thursday, 12 November — The cost of a wrong verb

The International Relations Coordinator and partnerships team sorted the fictional inquiry’s paperwork by document number, not by the order in which the pages had arrived. They distinguished a transfer reference from the contents of a file, an acknowledgment from understanding, and understanding from permission.

At the same time, the pinboard carried real-world headlines reported in the supplied research: Ghana’s 2027 budget, business and market developments, and a disruption affecting widely used Microsoft communications services. The Legal Journalist left those items as reports to be checked and followed; no one claimed they were incidents at Koware or proof about the Kuala Lumpur notices.

The distinctions converged on one practical question: if a message cannot be authenticated, what would make it responsible to forward it as an instruction?

The file remained unopened. Its contents remained unknown.

### Friday, 13 November — The message that stayed unsent

The Events & Tours Coordinator noticed a report that the Ecobank JoyBusiness SME Fair was opening in Accra. The Community Outreach Officer asked whether participation would be accessible. The Booking Agent looked at the proposed attendance note.

“Those names mean they’d like to go,” the Booking Agent said. “They don’t mean we have a booking.”

The Events Coordinator kept the visit as a proposal. No one was added to a bus, a guest list, or a confirmed schedule.

The Booking Agent then tried to slide an overfilled folder under the meeting-room door. It wedged halfway. As the folder sprang open, one loose sheet landed squarely on the Agent’s head.

That was the morning’s one slapstick mishap. The scattered papers were gathered, sorted, and returned to their proper files.

At the briefing, Edliex—Koware’s AI, pronounced “Alex”—had listened while the desks reported. Only after the reports did the Corporate Manager invite it to give feedback. Edliex’s indicator shifted to feedback mode.

“Each desk encountered a document that could travel farther than its authority,” Edliex said. “A report is not a finding. An announcement is not a Koware contract. A distribution record is not an approval.”

The indicator changed again: conversational mode.

“What can we do?” the manager asked.

“Build a trace,” Edliex replied. “Record the source, who may approve it, which revision applies, whether it fits the present situation, and where each version went. Do not turn an unanswered field into an answer.”

Someone proposed a message to Kuala Lumpur: *Use the direction repeated by three notices.*

The Corporate Manager read it once and deleted it.

The team drafted a request for verification instead. It asked the proper custodian to preserve the copies, route the request through the responsible office, and seek qualified local guidance on the current arrangement. The request remained in the unsent queue pending approval and an authenticated channel. No route was selected. No sealed box was opened. No new field contact, mission movement, or Edubeam capability was established.

On the reception board, the Legal Journalist left the week’s developing headlines visible for continued checking. Beneath them, the blank space remained.

The four notices had not become trustworthy through repetition. The request had not become an instruction because it was well worded. And the missing name had not appeared simply because the team needed one.

At closing, the message was still unsent.

### Next episode preview

The next episode begins with the verification request still awaiting authorization. The sealed record, the notices’ provenance, and the question of which guidance applies to the present building remain unresolved.

Several copies of an urgent notice disagree, and a distribution record does not show who approved the instruction. What should the team do before treating any version as authoritative?

Below is a **provisional consolidated episode draft** based on the archive material and the prior episode-response file. # Classies Chronicles

## Season 00 — Foundation Phase  
### Chapter 05 — The Signal Before Sunrise  
## Episode 02 — The Authority Behind the Arrow

**Coverage:** 2–13 November 2026  
**Editorial status:** Provisional consolidated draft; awaiting human review  
**Working identifier:** `S00_CH05_E02` — retained as the existing archive-style identifier; formal calendar and crosswalk approval are not established.

### Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

Before the New Legon Estates compound had fully stirred, Enka had already written three questions in his notebook:

**What does the notice say?**  
**Where did it come from?**  
**Who had authority to issue it?**

The last question mattered most.

At the Koware Group’s New Legon Estates headquarters, four photographs arrived from the field inquiry in Kuala Lumpur. They showed four emergency notices in Malay, English, Mandarin and Tamil. Three arrows pointed toward one stairwell. The fourth pointed elsewhere.

The images were clear enough to show the disagreement. They were not clear enough to settle it.

“The odd one is lower on the wall,” the R&D Lab Specialist said, enlarging the picture. “It also has a date.”

“Could be newer,” said the Liaison Technician. “Could be an older form that still has its date.”

Enka studied the four images. Similar layouts could make the notices look like independent witnesses, but they might all have been copied from one source. A date might help identify a version; it did not prove that the version was valid.

“Record what the photographs show,” he said. “Keep that separate from what we think the signs mean.”

The specialist placed four small magnetic arrows on a metal-edged tray to mark the directions. One stuck to the tray; the others followed it in a rattling procession and attached themselves to Enka’s cuff. He looked down at the little line of arrows on his sleeve, then carefully lifted them off one by one.

The brief laughter passed. The questions remained.

The field team’s assignment was observation and documentation. No one was to test either route or offer guidance to the building’s occupants. Enka asked for the original, uncropped photographs and the notice versions to be preserved without alteration.

Four languages had carried the message across a building. They had not yet carried its authority with it.

### Part 2: The Cropped Truth

The next set of images arrived cropped.

The fourth notice was visible. So was the lift. The wall around them—the part that might have shown whether the notice had been moved, replaced or displayed beside another version—was missing.

At the headquarters, Enka asked the Human & Employment Resources Manager and the Health & Safety Technician to review what the image could and could not establish. The team requested the uncropped original, its creation date, and any current building arrangements that the responsible local personnel were prepared to share.

The building manager described a familiar chain: one person might request a notice, another prepare the wording, someone else handle translation, and another staff member display the finished sheet. The sheet on the wall did not reveal the whole chain.

At the same time, a handwritten sign inside the New Legon compound had been taped too high on a storage-room door. A cleaning trolley caught its loose corner, and the sign fluttered down onto the Janitor’s overalls. He looked at the words **KEEP THIS AREA CLEAR**, then at the cloths in his arms.

“I see they have already made their decision about me,” he said, peeling the paper away.

The staff laughed, then moved the trolley clear of the doorway.

The H&S Technician used the small mishap to make a practical point: an instruction has to be legible and appropriately placed, but even a perfectly placed warning cannot tell people what to do if it conflicts with the responsible safety plan.

The complete image came back later. It showed a faint line beneath the fourth arrow, but not clearly enough to authenticate the wording.

The crop had hidden a question. The full image restored it without answering it.

### Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

The building manager’s reply came with a distribution register, not the original instruction.

It listed copies and where they had been placed. It showed that the notices had circulated. It did not name the person who approved the instruction or establish that the translations matched an approved original.

Enka drew two columns on the meeting-room board:

**WHAT THIS RECORD SHOWS**  
**WHAT THIS RECORD DOES NOT SHOW**

The register went under the first heading. The missing approval went under the second.

“Silence about a signature doesn’t prove there was no signature,” the specialist said. “It means this register doesn’t supply one.”

The field team had not followed either direction or tested a stairwell. Enka told the group to preserve the register alongside the photographs and to seek the original instruction, its approval history, and the versions that had been issued.

A printer in the nearby office began producing the register’s pages in reverse order. The last sheet slid off the tray first and landed over Bonsu Bolt’s visitor badge. He lifted it, read the badge still visible beneath the paper, and gave the printer a look usually reserved for people who had ignored a clear instruction.

Nobody mistook the printer’s behaviour for evidence. The pages were sorted, checked and filed.

The next request was narrow: find the source instruction and any record showing who approved it, when it took effect, and which versions were distributed. The team would not decide which route was safe.

The register was a real lead in the story. It was not the answer.

### Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

At the R&D console, the Millennium Probe’s displayed date differed from earlier archived readings.

Enka asked the specialist to preserve the new reading with its inputs and acquisition context, rather than replacing what had come before. The historical-reference, production and Probe readings belonged in separate records. None established the field team’s individual location or temporal state.

A loose label on a technical binder peeled away in the room’s air-conditioning and landed squarely on Enka’s notebook. He lifted it by one corner and placed it beside the relevant record.

“Even the label needs a source,” he said.

The conversation returned to the notices. The manager had not found the original instruction. A photograph of the line beneath an arrow was still too indistinct to authenticate. The group kept the question open rather than using the changing Probe display to explain the field evidence.

Enka opened an older design sheet. He could describe the Probe’s history as it appeared in the source material, but the team still needed the actual records and the accepted technology canon before treating an explanation as established.

“Keep the history beside the readings,” he said. “Not in place of them.”

Two inquiries now sat side by side: the authority behind the notice and the interpretation of the changing Probe readings. Neither was allowed to stand in for the other.

The dates on the display were precise. Their meaning still needed evidence.

### Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

By Friday, the four notice versions had been compared repeatedly. Three appeared to agree. The fourth differed.

James Evans, Gloria Mica and Moses Larbi reviewed the papers from their respective academic, coordination and quality-assurance perspectives. The question was not which page had the most supporters. It was whether any of the pages could be traced to an authoritative source.

“Three agreeing copies can still share one mistake,” Gloria said. “We need to know whether they came from the same master.”

Moses recorded the possible hazard without declaring that any route was safe or unsafe. James asked that each version be retained and that the next communication explain the uncertainty plainly.

A ceiling fan lifted the unfastened corners of the four sheets in sequence. For a moment they all bowed forward together, like a row of very serious delegates agreeing to something. Moses placed a paperweight across them before the pages could slide off the table.

The laughter was brief. The papers stayed in order.

The team agreed that a distribution record, a translation, a date or a majority of copies could each support a limited claim. None alone proved that an instruction was authorized and current.

The investigation had not found its source. It had found a more exact question:

**What did the agreement among three copies depend on?**

### Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

The next Monday, Johnson Ortsin unfolded a site plan in a meeting room. Derek Shiraska marked a boundary on the work plan: paper review was within scope; a physical rehearsal of the disputed routes was not.

“If three copies say east, we have a majority,” Johnson said.

“A majority of paper,” Derek replied. “Not a verified exit.”

Matthias Agbenorku reviewed the operations notes. The documents pointed to **Work Order 44-E** and an older records transfer. A possible storage box had been located, but no one had opened it and nobody knew what it contained.

Johnson reached for a site-plan roll. Its elastic band sprang loose; the roll unfurled across the table and flicked a teaspoon into Matthias’s empty cup.

“We have identified the box,” Derek said, catching the roll before it swept the remaining papers aside.

“We have identified where the box is,” Johnson corrected.

The correction changed the next step. The team prepared a request to the responsible records and safety authorities for the source instruction, the applicable approval record and the current site plan. They did not conduct a route test or issue a temporary instruction.

A separate maintenance notice prompted a check of whether any listed work would affect Koware’s own facilities. It was treated as a reason to verify, not as proof that a particular building would lose power.

The sealed-box lead gave the inquiry somewhere to look. It did not give anyone permission to open it.

### Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The release request looked nearly complete.

It had a transfer reference, a date and a stamp. The line for the person authorized to request the file was blank.

Mubarak Mohsen aligned the form with the transfer record. The two pages confirmed different things: one recorded a transfer; the other sought a release. Neither established that the right person had asked for it.

On the table, Elijah Peter’s arranged the envelopes without opening the correspondence inside. Lovemore Saiga brought in a faded plant label from the garden.

“Accurate date,” he said, turning the label over. “No idea what it belongs to.”

He set it beside the form. It was an apt comparison, though the label did not solve the paperwork.

Enka brought up a draft message to the field team:

*We have found the authorizing instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Mubarak stopped him.

“We have found a possible container for the instruction. The box is still sealed.”

Enka deleted the sentence. The revised request asked the proper custodian to confirm the authorized requester and the release process. It also asked that qualified local personnel confirm what guidance applied to the building as it stood now.

Mubarak signed only as the preparer of a draft request. His signature could not grant authority he did not have.

A second question remained: even if the original instruction was recovered, did it still match the present building?

The authorization line stayed blank.

### Part 8: The Message That Stayed Unsent

#### Wednesday, 11 November

At the reception pinboard, the Legal Journalist drew a line through **CONFIRMED** on a draft card and replaced it with **REPORTED**.

Josh Lafa, the Public Relations & Press Coordinator, read the draft description of the Kuala Lumpur notices. It said that the instructions had been approved. The records supported distribution, not approval.

Terry Joan, the Marketing Manager, withdrew a headline that made the case sound solved. The team could describe the inquiry; it could not present an unverified direction as an official safety instruction.

The Legal Journalist’s marker ran dry halfway through **REPORTED**. The word ended as a thin, fading line. The journalist shook the marker once, tested it on the margin, and rewrote the label clearly.

A blank rectangle was left on the board:

**AUTHORITY NOT YET VERIFIED.**

Around it, dated items from Ghana and elsewhere were marked for source and status checks. The board was meant to help staff distinguish developing reports from confirmed information, not to imply that every headline was a Koware contract, decision or threat.

#### Thursday, 12 November

The International Relations and Global Partnerships colleagues compared the wording and provenance of the records. They kept the Malaysian notice inquiry distinct from any unrelated news concerning Malaysia.

A receipt could show that a request had arrived. It could not prove that the recipient understood it or that the sender had authority to ask for the file. The team drafted a secondary contact route and an offline record of decisions in case the ordinary channel failed.

The registrar added a careful annotation beside the box reference:

*Location indicated; contents unknown; release authority unresolved.*

No fresh authenticated field report established the individual team members’ conditions or whereabouts. The supplied geographic position remained Kuala Lumpur; it did not answer the other questions.

The annotation did not solve the mystery. It prevented the mystery from being mistaken for a solution.

#### Friday, 13 November

At the Friday meeting, the Legal Journalist reviewed the pinboard and its developing headlines. The Events & Tours Coordinator and Community Outreach Officer discussed a possible visit to a local small-business fair. The Booking Agent kept expressed interest separate from a confirmed arrangement.

As the meeting began, the Booking Agent tried to slide an overfilled folder beneath the door while balancing attendance forms. The folder jammed, the forms lifted into the air, and one landed over the agent’s face like a paper mask. The room laughed; the papers were gathered and the meeting continued.

Then Enka returned the group to the Kuala Lumpur file. Four notices. Three matching arrows. One different. A distribution record. Work Order 44-E. A sealed box. No verified approval.

Edliex had listened through the first part of the meeting. Enka invited its feedback. The system’s status light changed.

“Across the notice inquiry and today’s planning, we keep encountering the same distinctions,” Edliex said. “A record of receipt is not endorsement. A record of distribution is not authorization. A location reference is not proof of contents.”

Enka invited Edliex into conversational mode.

The AI proposed a verification sequence: establish the original source; identify the approving authority; verify the revision and effective date; compare the instruction with the current site plan; check the translations against the approved source; and have the responsible local safety authority determine what guidance applies.

“Would that let us announce a safe stairwell?” Derek asked.

“No,” Edliex replied. “It would let us explain what evidence we have. The responsible qualified local authority must make that determination.”

A draft sentence appeared on the screen:

*Use the direction shown on the three matching notices pending further advice.*

Enka read it once and deleted it.

The replacement request sought authorization to inspect the archive through a verified channel, asked for the source and revision history, and requested current guidance from the responsible local personnel. The sender-authorization and channel-authentication fields were still incomplete.

No one pressed **SEND**.

The box remained sealed. The original instruction remained unauthenticated. The team remained within its observation-only assignment. The Probe readings remained a separate technical question.

The message stayed unsent because it claimed more than the evidence could support.

### Preview: Episode 03 — The Custodian’s Key

The inquiry may receive a response from the records custodian: perhaps a properly authorized request, perhaps a new condition that delays access. Even if the box is opened, its contents will still need to be authenticated and compared with the building’s current arrangements.

## The Classies Challenge — Episode 02

A distribution register confirms that four translated notices were circulated, and a transfer record points to a sealed box that may contain an original instruction. Neither establishes that the displayed directions are authorized and current.

**What must be verified before anyone can responsibly treat one of those directions as the building’s current emergency instruction?**
