# CLASSIES CHRONICLES  
## The Authority Behind the Arrow

**Internal editorial compilation — provisional draft, not approved for publication**

### Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

Four notices arrived at the New Legon office before the morning briefing had properly begun.

They carried the same warning in four languages. The layout was so similar that, at first glance, the pages seemed to confirm one another. But three arrows pointed toward an east stairwell. The fourth pointed toward a courtyard corridor.

Enka placed the photographs side by side. The Koware Group’s headquarters occupied five single-storey apartment blocks clustered together in New Legon Estates, each block assigned to a different department. From one block to the next, the offices were near enough to share a boundary—but that did not make their records, responsibilities or decisions interchangeable.

“Four languages,” someone said. “Three copies agree.”

“Four pages,” Enka replied. “We still don’t know where the instruction began.”

A clerk tried to line up the photographs with a ruler, but the ruler’s metal edge caught the corner of one sheet and slid it neatly beneath the others. For a moment the “fourth” notice disappeared. The clerk lifted the stack again, more carefully this time.

The laugh was brief. The disagreement was not.

The question was no longer simply which arrow looked most convincing. It was whether any of the copies could be traced to an original instruction, an authorized source and a plan that still matched the building. Nobody at the Accra table could safely choose a route for a facility in Kuala Lumpur.

Enka left all four photographs untouched and asked the team to preserve the originals and establish who was responsible for the building’s current safety arrangements.

### Part 2: The Cropped Truth

The next image seemed to settle the argument.

A close crop showed three matching arrows, clean and unmistakable. The fourth notice barely appeared at the edge. The image had travelled farther than the full set of photographs, and its confident caption had travelled with it.

Then a wider version arrived.

Below the arrow was a line the crop had removed. It might identify a revision, a location or something else entirely; the image was not clear enough to say. But its presence changed the question. The crop had not necessarily falsified the notice. It had made a fragment look like the whole.

The team compared the available versions without filling in the unreadable line. One of the paper copies had curled around the corner of the comparison board and briefly hid the cropped image itself. The person presenting it had to unpin the papers and start again.

“No conclusion from a picture that has cut away what we need to inspect,” Enka said.

The request sent back to Kuala Lumpur was specific: preserve the uncropped original, record when and where each image was obtained, and ask the building’s responsible personnel for the current approved emergency arrangements. No one was asked to test a route or infer the missing line.

### Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

The reply brought a register, not the original instruction.

It listed the four versions and recorded their distribution. That mattered. It showed that copies had moved through a process. It did not establish who approved the instruction they carried, whether the translations matched an authorized master or whether the directions remained suitable for the building.

Enka divided a page into two headings: **WHAT THE REGISTER SHOWS** and **WHAT IT DOES NOT SHOW**.

The distribution belonged beneath the first. Approval and present-day safety belonged beneath the second.

Bonsu “Bolt,” the Concierge Liaison, asked what a visitor should do if the posted directions disagreed. The answer was not for the Accra team to invent. The building’s qualified local personnel had to determine what current guidance applied.

While the register was being copied, the office printer fed its pages out in reverse order. The last sheet landed face down across Bonsu’s visitor badge, briefly making his nameplate read **APPENDIX FOUR**. He turned it around and put it back where it belonged.

The laughter stopped when the team returned to the unanswered question: who had authorized the instruction?

A reference in the records pointed toward an older file transfer. The file itself was not attached. The investigation had gained a trail, but not a signature.

### Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

By Thursday, a second uncertainty had appeared on the laboratory display.

A new Probe reading showed a different future-dated coordinate from earlier snapshots. The values were logged separately; none, by itself, established that anyone had moved through time or that the deployed team’s position had changed. The historical reference, the production present and the Probe reading remained three different records with three different meanings.

Enka looked at the date, then at the earlier entries. In the story’s working account, he had designed the original Probe and established the cloned Edubeam, whose initial operation had been handled by the Micro Computer Technology Institute of London. But even a designer’s explanation could not substitute for the raw readings and their calculation history.

“Preserve the old readings,” he said. “Don’t overwrite them with the latest one.”

A printout came off the lab printer with the newest timestamp tucked behind an older page. Obed Ali, the R&D Lab Specialist, separated the sheets and restored them to their proper order. The small mishap made the room smile; it did not make the readings agree.

The team kept the Probe question separate from the notice investigation. A changing coordinate did not identify who issued an emergency instruction. Neither a timestamp nor a destination could authorize anyone to test the disputed route.

The mystery now had two trails: the origin of the notices and the meaning of the shifting readings. Neither was resolved by guessing.

### Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

Four documents lay on James Evans’s table. The Director of Academic Programs studied their wording while Gloria Mica, the Faculty Coordinator, compared the language versions.

“Agreement isn’t the same as independence,” Gloria said. “Three translations could all repeat the same mistake if they came from one incorrect source.”

Moses Larbi, the Quality Assurance & Compliance Manager, recorded the distinction. The safety question was not a vote. Choosing the most common arrow could turn an unverified assumption into advice people might rely on.

The group’s practical response was to preserve the four versions, trace each one to its source, and keep the unresolved discrepancy visible. The available Health & Safety material identified HS1000, Topic 3, on proactive safety, hazard identification and risk management; it offered a useful frame for taking uncertainty seriously, not a basis for claiming that the course had settled the building’s procedures.

Enka recalled Philippians 4:8, identified in the source materials as the YouVersion reading for that day, and considered the discipline of seeking what was true before repeating what was convenient. The thought did not select an arrow. It strengthened the reason not to pretend that consensus among copies was proof.

As the four pages were stacked for safekeeping, one slid sideways and ended up upside down on top of the others. James turned it around before the stack could be filed. Even the papers seemed determined to remind them that order and authority were different things.

The documents agreed in places. Their common source remained unknown.

### Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

The Monday review began with a plan to organize the next steps.

Johnson Ortsin, the Programme Manager, had arranged the outstanding work in sequence. Derek Shiraska, the Production Coordinator, had separated what could be documented from what must not be tested without authority. Matthias Agbenorku, the Director of Operations, brought the operations notes and the latest account from Kuala Lumpur.

A work order marked **44-E** appeared to record that the notices had been printed and distributed. It did not establish who had authorized the underlying instruction. A records office had also traced an older transfer to a sealed box. Its location had been identified; its contents had not.

“So we found the box,” Johnson said.

“We found where it is,” Derek replied.

Matthias reached for a rolled site plan. Its elastic sprang loose and the plan opened across the table, nudging his empty cup onto its side with a clatter. Enka lifted the cup, checked that it had spilled nothing, and said, “At least that plan has no tea to spill.”

When the laughter faded, the team returned to the real boundary: no route rehearsal, no walk-through based on the conflicting notices, and no new emergency instructions from the Accra office. Qualified local personnel had to determine what applied at the facility.

Johnson revised the schedule. The first task was not rehearsal; it was to verify the source instruction, the current site plan and the authority behind the notices.

The box remained sealed. The arrow remained unendorsed.

### Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The release request looked nearly complete. It had a transfer reference, a date and a stamp. The space for the authorized requester was blank.

Mubarak Mohsen, the Facility Manager, aligned the form with the transfer record. The papers established that a box had been moved and where it might be found. They did not give the custodian permission to release it.

Outside, Lovemore Saiga, the Landscape Gardener, brought in a plant label whose date remained readable after watering had blurred the name. Elijah Peter’s, the Facility Butler, looked at it and said, “We know when it was planted. We don’t know what it is.”

As Elijah reached to set it down, the damp label slipped onto his shoulder and sat there like a tiny badge reading **IDENTITY REQUIRED**. He stood at attention for a beat while Lovemore retrieved it. Mubarak’s frown softened.

Enka drafted a message for the Kuala Lumpur team: *We have found the authorizing instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Mubarak stopped him.

“We have found a sealed box that might contain a relevant file. We have not found the instruction.”

Enka deleted the sentence. The replacement asked the proper custodian, through an authenticated channel, who could request the file. It also asked the responsible local personnel to confirm current guidance. The team’s supplied geographic position remained Kuala Lumpur, but no one claimed fresh confirmation of each person’s whereabouts or condition.

The request went forward for review, not as an order. The name line stayed blank until the right authority could fill it.

### Part 8: The Message That Stayed Unsent

The Legal Journalist changed one word on the reception pinboard.

**CONFIRMED** became **REPORTED**.

Across the five blocks, the board was a visible record of what staff were discussing. The Legal Journalist’s role called for sourced reporting, careful distinctions between fact and interpretation, and clear corrections; the job description and governance materials available for review were drafts, not proof of adopted delegations. The word change was an editorial caution, not a corporate ruling.

The Public Relations & Press Coordinator and Marketing Manager withdrew language that made the Kuala Lumpur notices sound approved. The International Relations team kept that fictional facility inquiry separate from unrelated reporting about Malaysia. Same country, different subject, different evidence.

On Wednesday, the board’s blank rectangle read: **AUTHORITY NOT YET VERIFIED.**

On Thursday, the international desk kept three questions apart: What was the source? Who could authorize it? Did the old instruction still match the building as it stood? Acknowledgment of a message was not proof that its recipient understood or had authority to act on it.

The headquarters also received a maintenance notice listing parts of New Legon for work later in the week. The team noted it for readiness checks, without claiming that the notice proved a disruption at Koware’s particular premises.

By Friday, the Legal Journalist had pinned a small set of reported headlines from the source chronicles: Ghana’s 2027 Budget, the SME Fair at Accra Mall, discussion of digital-market infrastructure, and a Malaysian investigation into possible AI-chip re-exports. The wording preserved the distinction between an investigation and an established finding. Events & Tours Coordinator Thomisia Daniel and Community Outreach Officer Ingrid Razak discussed a possible observational visit. Booking Agent Ceda Bertto kept expressions of interest separate from confirmed arrangements. Nothing was booked or approved in that conversation.

Ceda tried to push an overfilled folder under the meeting-room door while holding loose attendance sheets. The folder jammed; one sheet landed over Ceda’s head like a paper helmet stamped **CONFIRMED ATTENDANCE**. Thomisia lifted it away. “A very clear label,” she said, “on a thoroughly unconfirmed situation.”

Then the meeting turned back to the four notices.

Edliex had listened through the opening reports. When Enka invited its feedback, the listening indicator changed.

“The available records establish distribution,” Edliex said. “They do not establish authorization, translation accuracy or whether the directions remain applicable to the current building.”

Enka asked what the next step should be.

“Separate the questions,” Edliex replied. “Establish the source. Establish the authority. Establish the version in force. Establish whether it still matches the building. Then check whether each translation preserves the authorized instruction.”

Three agreeing copies, Edliex added, proved a property of the copies—not the authority or accuracy of their common source.

The meeting ended with all four versions preserved and the original instruction still missing. The message that would have told the team to proceed was never sent.

### Preview of the Next Episode

The next episode begins with the records search still open: a sealed box, an incomplete chain of authority and a team that has not been told to test either route. The next discovery must answer what the documents show—not what the investigators hope they will show.

### Classies Challenge

Four versions of an emergency notice have been distributed. Some agree, but the original instruction, its approval and its current applicability remain unverified. What should the team establish before anyone treats one of the directions as authoritative?

- No new family, romantic, medical, private-diary or other sensitive character facts are added.
- Character-canon and portrayal approval remain unresolved. No visual collage or character file was changed.

### B4 — Mission and technology state

- **Central investigation:** Four emergency notices have conflicting directions. Distribution is evidenced in the story; authorization, translation lineage, current applicability and responsible approving authority remain unresolved.
- **Work Order 44-E:** Evidence of distribution, not proof of authorization.
- **Sealed box:** Location/transfer lead only; contents remain unknown and unopened.
- **Requester:** The release request has an unresolved authority/name field. No one is represented as having authority to open or release the records.
- **Field team:** Kuala Lumpur remains the supplied geographic position in the story materials; current member-by-member roster, condition and temporal position are not authenticated in this compilation.
- **Safety boundary:** No route is selected or tested by the Accra team. Current directions must be determined by qualified local personnel responsible for the building.
- **Probe:** Readings are preserved as distinct observations; no physical movement or Edubeam capability is inferred from a changed timestamp.
- **Next mission state:** No departure, retrieval, return, successful contact, intervention or mission resolution is established.

### B5 — Append-only continuity and story ledger

**Accepted pre-state:** No human-accepted episode ledger or accepted revision was verified as part of this compilation. Earlier daily narratives are used as supplied story material, not automatically promoted to accepted canon.

**Proposed episode events, if editorially accepted:**

1. Four multilingual emergency notices carry conflicting directional information.
2. A cropped image obscures a line needed for interpretation.
3. A distribution register and Work Order 44-E document circulation but do not establish authorization.
4. Copies that agree may share a source; agreement alone does not establish independent corroboration.
5. Probe timestamps vary across source snapshots; no temporal travel or team movement is inferred.
6. A sealed records box is located through a transfer reference but remains unopened.
7. A release request lacks an established authorized requester.
8. The Legal Journalist marks a claim as reported rather than confirmed and keeps unrelated Malaysian news separate from the fictional notice inquiry.
9. Edliex listens to the meeting, provides feedback and then enters conversational mode, recommending separate verification of source, authority, version, current applicability and translation.
10. The proposed message to proceed is withheld; the investigation remains open.

**Open storylines:** Who authored and approved the original instruction? What does the sealed box contain? Who can authorize its release? Do the translations faithfully reflect an approved source? Does any historic instruction match the current building? What do the changing Probe readings mean?

**Relationship updates:** No new relationship status is proposed. The scenes show work disagreements and corrections only; they do not establish lasting personal conflict or intimacy.

**Slapstick count:** One brief physical-comedy beat is included in each of Parts 1–8. Part 8 consolidates its three source dates and contains one beat for that consolidated source installment. No additional slapstick is added within those parts.

**Acceptance rule:** All events above remain proposed until an authorized editor accepts them. No accepted-state projection or canon promotion is claimed.

### B6 — Portable production handover

The Kuala Lumpur team position and Las Vegas historical-reference setting are carried only as provided by the source narratives. Probe timestamps remain separate.

# CLASSIES CHRONICLES  
## Season 00 — Foundation Phase  
### Chapter 05 — The Signal Before Sunrise  
# Episode 02 — The Authority Behind the Arrow

**Working identity:** `S00_CH05_E02`  
**Episode period:** Monday, 2 November–Friday, 13 November 2026  
**Edition:** Provisional consolidated episode draft — not approved for publication  
**Structure:** Eight archived installments shaped into ten working-day parts. The final archive installment covers the closing three days.

---

## Part 1: The Notice in Four Languages

Before the city had fully shaken off the night’s rain, four notices waited beside a lift in Kuala Lumpur.

They carried the same warning in four languages. Three arrows pointed toward one stairwell. The fourth pointed across the lobby toward a corridor. A date appeared beneath that arrow, but the photograph was too soft to show what the date referred to—or who had written it.

The Classies field analyst photographed the notices from the permitted observation point. The field lead repeated the limits of the assignment: observe, record, preserve. No route would be tested. No instruction would be issued to the building’s occupants.

In New Legon, Accra, Enka studied the images at Koware. The five single-story apartment blocks that housed the Group’s departments shared boundary lines; for this review, staff gathered in the administrative block and kept the incoming evidence on a screen, not on a wall where a passer-by might mistake it for guidance.

“Three against one,” someone said.

“Three copies against one copy,” Enka replied. “We haven’t established what any of them can prove.”

At the Research and Development desk, Gifty Bright asked the lab specialist and liaison technician to separate what the photographs showed from what the team had heard. The count of notices, their languages and the directions of the arrows went in one column. The date, the issuer and the reason for the disagreement went in another.

On the table, four small magnetic arrows were arranged for comparison. One snapped onto the metal document tray. The other three followed with a sharp little clatter, leaving the analyst staring at a sudden “majority” attached to the wrong object.

The laughter was brief. The warning was not. An apparent consensus could be manufactured by copying, and even a clear photograph could not confer authority.

Enka wrote the questions that would guide the inquiry: Who issued the original instruction? Who was authorized to approve it? Did each translation accurately reflect that approved source? And did any version match the building as it stood now?

The analyst requested the complete original photographs and the facility’s current approved arrangements through the appropriate local channel. The field team stayed where it was.

Four languages had carried a message through a building. They had not yet established who had the right to send it.

---

## Part 2: The Cropped Truth

The next image arrived with its lower edge missing.

The crop made the fourth notice look cleaner and more decisive than it had in the lobby. It removed the faint line beneath the arrow—the line that might have been a date, a revision note or something else entirely.

At the Human and Employment Resources desk, Josephine Ohemeng asked the Health and Safety technician to review the image alongside the earlier photographs. Esther Justine and Cornelius Jekora, working nearby, were sorting a stack of storage-room notices when a trolley’s wheel caught the edge of a loose mat. The trolley stopped; the carefully stacked notices slid a few inches, but stayed on the table.

“Even a notice has to be placed where people can read it,” the technician said, setting the papers straight. “And first we have to know what it actually says.”

The comment brought the Kuala Lumpur problem back into focus. Legibility mattered, but it could not settle the source or meaning of a safety-critical instruction.

Enka asked the team to preserve both images—the cropped copy and the uncropped original—and to record which had arrived first. He requested the creation date and source of the original photograph, along with a copy of the current building arrangements from the responsible office. Nobody was to enhance or redraw the missing line and then treat the result as evidence.

The Eternal Dreamfall study list identified its Week 96 selection as the Septuagint’s Leviticus and Numbers. The precise reading for that date was not established. Enka did not invent one. He reflected instead on transmission: a message might pass from one language to another, from one desk to another, and from one copy to the next, while each handoff concealed some part of its history.

The day’s verse was Psalm 119:11. Enka thought about the responsibility of keeping what is true close enough to guide a decision—and not allowing a convenient fragment to take the place of the whole record.

The building manager was asked to identify who had requested the notices, who had prepared the original wording, who had authorized translations, and when each version had been posted. The response was cautious: several people might have been involved, and the manager needed to check the records.

No one in Koware filled the gap with a guess.

By day’s end, the cropped image had become useful for a different reason. It showed exactly how much the missing edge mattered.

---

## Part 3: The Unsigned Fourth Notice

The reply came with a register, not the original instruction.

It listed the four versions and recorded their distribution. That was evidence that copies had moved through the building. It did not show who approved the source, whether the translations matched it or whether the directions remained current.

Isaac Barry, Technical Support and Operations Manager, arranged for the register and the photographs to be preserved together as separate records. Bonsu Bolt, working at the concierge desk, checked the visitor log and found no entry that identified the notice’s issuer. His badge slipped from beneath the register as he lifted it; the badge landed face down over the column marked “Authorizing source.”

For a moment, the empty column had a cover.

Then Bonsu turned the badge over, and the blank was still blank.

Enka divided a page into two headings: **What this record shows** and **What this record does not show**. The first held distribution. The second still held approval, authorship, translation lineage, effective date and current applicability.

The field lead confirmed that no one had followed either arrow. The team had not approached occupants or tested an exit. Any assessment of current routes belonged to the qualified local people responsible for the building.

The day’s safety discussion settled on a practical distinction: an inconsistent instruction may be a hazard to investigate, but that recognition does not authorize an outside observer to choose a route. The relevant lesson for the episode is hazard identification and risk assessment, followed by controls and communication through the people with the proper responsibility.

The day’s assigned Koware roles included technical support and operations. In the story, that work meant protecting the record: preserve the versions, retain their provenance, and do not silently correct a document whose authority remains unknown.

Enka asked for the original source instruction, the approval record, its effective date and the records that linked each distributed version to it. He did not mark the notice genuine or false.

The register had answered one question. It had made the remaining ones harder to ignore.

---

## Part 4: The Instruction No One Could Sign

At the R&D console, the Millennium Probe displayed another date.

An earlier stored reading and the new display did not match. The academic staff wanted one date to put on a training slide. Obed Ali, the R&D Lab Specialist, brought up the run record instead: historical reference, production timestamp, phase details and the Probe output, each in its own field.

“The number is precise,” Enka said. “That doesn’t tell us what it authorizes.”

“Or whether it tells us where the field team is,” Obed replied.

The Probe reading, the team’s supplied geographic position in Kuala Lumpur and the team members’ individual whereabouts were not interchangeable. No one treated a timestamp as proof of travel or a changed mission state. The earlier and later readings were preserved rather than overwritten.

At the academic desk, James Evans, Steven Sarpong and Matthew Ebenezer reviewed how a record could be used in teaching without turning a measured output into a settled explanation. Gloria Mica and Moses Larbi pressed the same point from coordination and quality assurance: distinguish the source, its interpretation and the unknowns.

A loose label peeled away from the cover of a training binder and stuck to the edge of the console. Obed removed it and set it beside—not over—the current run sheet.

The distinction also applied to the four-language notice. The distribution register showed circulation, not the identity of the approving authority. The Probe showed a reading, not the field team’s temporal position. Neither file could answer the question posed by the other.

Enka asked the laboratory to keep the raw responses beside the earlier readings, with the time and source of each clearly recorded. He asked the academic team not to put a single unqualified destination date on the slide.

Two questions remained open: who had authorized the notice, and what the changing Probe readings meant. The team had gained a method for asking both questions without pretending they were solved.

---

## Part 5: The Copies That Agreed

By Friday, the four notices had acquired a paper trail.

They had not acquired an author.

James Evans placed the versions beside one another. Gloria examined their wording and layout; Moses separated what each could establish from what had merely been repeated. Four languages did not necessarily mean four independent sources. Three copies might have descended from the same unverified original.

The week’s study of translation and transmission gave Enka a useful frame, but not a shortcut to certainty. The daily verse, Philippians 4:8, urged attention to what is true and worthy of careful thought. It did not identify which notice to trust.

Moses treated the disagreement as a possible safety hazard requiring appropriate assessment. The story’s practical safety focus remained hazard identification and risk assessment—not a claim that the characters had completed a particular course or that an outside team could prescribe a route.

The Legal Journalist placed a cautious item on the reception pinboard: **MULTILINGUAL SAFETY NOTICES — SOURCE AND APPROVAL UNCONFIRMED**. It carried the source and status, not a dramatic conclusion.

A ceiling fan lifted the unfastened corners of the four pages in sequence. They bowed forward together like a row of solemn delegates. Moses caught them before they slid off the table, and the meeting paused while Gloria found a clip.

The laughter lasted no longer than the interruption. The documents were still only documents.

The field team’s inquiry remained observational. Koware did not publish a safety instruction, conduct a route test or declare the majority correct. Enka asked the records liaison to pursue an older work-order reference that might connect the notices to an administrative transfer.

The team’s first week ended with more evidence and no verdict. The copies agreed in places. Their agreement did not tell the group whether they had independent origins, whether they reflected an authorized instruction, or whether any old instruction still applied.

---

## Part 6: The Line Beneath the Arrow

The second week began with Work Order 44-E.

Johnson Ortsin unfolded a site plan and began arranging the next steps. He wanted a demonstration at the end of the schedule.

Derek Shiraska drew a line through that item.

“We can rehearse the paperwork,” Derek said. “We cannot rehearse a disputed route in a building we don’t control.”

Johnson’s jaw tightened. He had organized the plan to make progress visible; Derek’s correction made the missing authority visible instead. Matthias Agbenorku, Director of Operations, checked the work order. It suggested a path by which the printed notices might have been installed. It did not establish who had approved their content.

Johnson’s plan roll sprang open across the table and flicked a teaspoon into Matthias’s empty cup. Matthias looked at the cup, then at the route plan stretching over the table edge. “Even the paperwork is trying to take a shortcut,” he said.

Johnson rolled the plan back up, more carefully this time.

The team revised its sequence: verify the source, establish who had authority to release it, compare any original with the current layout, then let qualified local personnel determine what guidance applied. A paper-based rehearsal could examine how the records were checked; it would not simulate people walking either route.

A separate Accra electricity-maintenance notice on the reception board prompted a practical check of its dates and affected areas. The staff did not assume that a general schedule proved a particular Koware building would lose power.

The Kuala Lumpur records office reported that 44-E led to an older transfer. A box associated with that transfer had been located.

“We found it,” Johnson said.

“We found where it is,” Derek corrected.

This time Johnson did not argue. The box’s location was a lead, not its contents, not permission to open it and not proof that it contained an authorized instruction.

The line beneath the arrow was still too faint to authenticate. The box was still closed. The schedule now reflected what the evidence allowed: a traceable next step, not a staged conclusion.

---

## Part 7: The Name the Paper Could Not Give

The release request had a date, a transfer reference and an official-looking stamp.

The line asking who was authorized to request the box was empty.

Mubarak Mohsen, Facility Manager, set the papers beside the records log. Elijah Peter’s arranged the envelopes without opening the correspondence inside. Lovemore Saiga brought in a tray of plant labels whose writing had faded after watering.

“Accurate date,” Lovemore said, pointing to one. “No idea what it belongs to.”

He set the tray down. The labels fanned across the desk and one settled on Elijah’s sleeve. Elijah lifted it away without disturbing the papers.

Enka had been preparing a message to Kuala Lumpur. Its first sentence read: *We have found the authorizing instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

Mubarak stopped him.

“We have found a possible container,” he said. “We haven’t seen what’s inside, and we don’t know who can request it.”

Enka deleted the sentence. He replaced it with a narrower draft: preserve the notices; ask the custodian which office may submit a valid request; seek current guidance from the responsible local safety personnel. It did not ask the field team to inspect or test anything.

A paper could show that a box had been transferred. It could not identify the person entitled to request its release. Even an authorized request would not establish that an old instruction still matched the building.

The group kept the production, historical-reference and Probe clocks in separate columns. The draft materials contained differing historical and future readings, none of which could establish the individual position or condition of the field team.

Mubarak marked the form **FOR AUTHORITY REVIEW**. His signature could record that he had prepared an internal request; it could not grant him authority over a foreign facility’s archive.

Enka left the proposed message unsent. The box remained sealed. The blank line had not delayed an authorized decision; it had prevented the group from pretending one had been made.

---

## Part 8: The Empty Space

On Wednesday, the Legal Journalist crossed out one word on the reception pinboard:

**CONFIRMED.**

Below it, in smaller letters, went **REPORTED**.

The board carried local and international headlines with their source, publication date and verification status. The Kuala Lumpur notice inquiry was identified as a separate fictional case, not a real incident or official safety advisory. The Public Relations & Press Coordinator and Marketing Manager removed language that implied approval had been established.

“Distributed is what the work order suggests,” the Press Coordinator said.

“Approved is what we are still trying to prove,” the Marketing Manager replied.

The debate was not about whether the story mattered. It was about whether a sentence on a public-facing board could outrun its evidence. They kept the case in the internal review and left a clear space for a later verified update.

Enka considered the day’s Bible App reference, Matthew 6:11, as a reminder to ask for what was needed now rather than claim possession of answers not yet found. He wrote a short note beneath the case summary: **Authority not yet verified.**

The note was not a finding about the building. It was an accurate description of the team’s knowledge.

The board’s digital version retained the source and date fields; the physical version carried the same careful wording. No one presented the disputed arrows as current instructions. The field team remained within its observation-only assignment.

A marker cap bounced off the edge of the pinboard and landed neatly in the empty coffee cup beside the desk. The journalist retrieved it, then returned to the unfinished headline.

The blank space stayed visible. It was not a conclusion. It was a boundary around what the newsroom could responsibly say.

---

## Part 9: The Cost of a Wrong Verb

By Thursday, the international desk had sorted the questions into separate records: source, approving authority, applicable revision, translation lineage, and the responsibility for checking current conditions.

The Global Partnerships Registrar organized the documents by their identifying details. The Global Partnerships Liaison tracked which language version came from which recorded source. Neither treated distribution as proof that recipients understood the notice.

“An acknowledgement is not comprehension,” the liaison said. “And comprehension is not permission.”

No authenticated report established the condition or individual whereabouts of every team member. The group’s supplied geographic position remained Kuala Lumpur; it did not substitute for a fresh, verified check-in.

On the reception board, the Legal Journalist prepared Ghana’s 2027 Budget item for verification as Parliament considered it. Another developing report concerned disruption to widely used Microsoft services. The newsroom treated both as separate news items, not evidence that Koware had received funding or suffered a service outage.

The International Relations desk discussed how a missing communication could leave an approval request in limbo. Its response was procedural: preserve a separate copy of the request and its status; do not mark it sent, approved or received unless the record showed that it was.

A follow-up annotation went beside the sealed-box reference: **Location indicated; contents unknown; release authority unresolved.**

The verse James 4:17 prompted Enka to think about the responsibility of acting when action was required. Restraint did not mean ignoring a possible safety concern. It meant that the request for current guidance had to reach the right people through an authorized channel—not become an instruction from those who lacked that authority.

The desks added a further question: even if an old instruction were recovered, did it still match the building as it stood now?

The file had not solved the mystery. It had made the cost of a wrong verb clearer.

---

## Part 10: The Message That Stayed Unsent

Friday’s pinboard carried the week’s headlines with their dates and sources. The Ghanaian budget had been presented. The Ecobank JoyBusiness SME Fair was opening at GHUD Park at Accra Mall. Reporting from Malaysia described an investigation into possible AI-chip re-exports; it did not establish a final finding. The newsroom kept that report separate from the fictional Kuala Lumpur notice case.

The Events & Tours Coordinator saw a possible opportunity for staff to observe the fair. The Community Outreach Officer wanted to learn what exhibitors and visitors might need. The Booking Agent opened a separate column for interest and confirmation.

“Those names mean they would like to go,” the Booking Agent said. “They don’t mean we have a booking.”

The Events Coordinator nodded. “Then the minutes will say proposed visit.”

The folder the Booking Agent tried to slide beneath the meeting-room door jammed halfway. Loose attendance sheets lifted in the draft from the open window; one settled over the agent’s head like a paper cap. The room laughed once, then the sheets were gathered and the unconfirmed names stayed unconfirmed.

At the meeting, Edliex—Koware’s AI, pronounced “Alex”—listened while the desks reported. It remained quiet through the first portion of the briefing. After Enka invited feedback, its status indicator changed.

“Each desk has met a version of the same problem,” Edliex said. “A report can be received without becoming a finding. A proposal can be discussed without becoming a commitment. A distribution record can show that copies moved without showing who authorized the instruction.”

“Then give us a way to trace it,” Enka said.

The indicator changed again, entering conversational mode.

Edliex proposed a sequence of checks: identify the source; identify the approving authority; verify which revision and date applied; compare the source with the current building arrangements; verify the translations against that source; and have the competent local authority determine current guidance.

“Would that let us announce a safe stairwell?” Derek asked.

“No,” Edliex replied. “It would show what the evidence can and cannot support. The responsible qualified local authority must determine current guidance.”

A draft message appeared on the screen: *Use the direction shown on the three matching notices until we hear otherwise.*

Enka read it once, then deleted it.

The words were confident. The evidence was not.

The group prepared a request for verification—not an operational command. It asked for the proper custodian, the applicable approval record and current guidance from the responsible local authority. But the sender’s authorization was still incomplete, and the channel had not been authenticated.

No one pressed **SEND**.

The request remained in the review queue. Work Order 44-E remained a distribution lead. The archive box remained sealed. The four notices still disagreed, and the field team had not been authorized to test either route.

That morning’s Eternal Dreamfall reading plan remained anchored in the Septuagint’s transmission and textual history; no daily passage was assigned. Philippians 4:8, chosen from the episode’s daily verses, had become the episode’s measure: seek what is true, right and worthy of careful thought.

Enka looked once more at the blank authorization field. The hardest decision of the week was not choosing a direction. It was refusing to send one before the evidence and authority were in place.

### Preview: Episode 03 — The Custodian’s Key

The sealed box has a location, but not yet an authorized request for release. The next inquiry may bring a custodian’s response—and a new question: even if the original instruction is found, will it still fit the building as it stands today?

### The Classies Challenge — Episode 02

A facility displays four translated emergency notices. A distribution record shows that copies circulated, and a sealed archive box may contain an original instruction. The issuing authority and the current building guidance remain unverified.

**What must be established before anyone can responsibly treat one of the displayed directions as the building’s current emergency instruction?**

---

# Episode Continuity Ledger & Production Handover  
**INTERNAL EDITORIAL WORKING RECORD — NOT PART OF PUBLIC OUTPUT A**

**Working episode:** `S00_CH05_E02`  
**Working title:** *The Authority Behind the Arrow*  
**Status:** Provisional narrative synthesis; unreviewed, not accepted as canon, not committed and not approved for publication.  
**Record boundary:** This ledger follows the public-facing episode here for editorial review. It must remain a separate internal artifact from any public release.

## B1 — Run and temporal manifest

- **Operation:** Episode-level editorial synthesis of the eight supplied archive installments into ten working-day parts. This does not certify that ten distinct daily source files or ten individually accepted chronicles exist.
- **Working identity:** `S00_CH05_E02`, Foundation Phase, Chapter 05, *The Signal Before Sunrise*. The archive contains a differently named working folder, *The Authority Behind the Arrow*. The formal identity/title crosswalk remains unratified.
- **Narrative dates:** 2–13 November 2026. Parts 1–5 cover 2–6 November; Parts 6–7 cover 9–10 November; Parts 8–10 cover 11–13 November as scenes within E02.08.
- **Calendar:** The source responses calculate the dates from a supplied phase boundary of 16 October 2026 and a first-Monday anchor of 19 October. This produces Episode 02’s proposed ten workdays from 2–13 November. No approved effective-dated calendar, production timezone or formal eight-archive/ten-day mapping was established in this review.
- **Source titles:** Archive folder titles used for the part headings. Alternative generated titles within source files are treated as draft variants, not silent renames of archive records.
- **Three clocks:** The source archives contain differing historical and Probe readings. They remain source-day observations; no single episode-wide H or F value is asserted. Production narrative dates are historical story dates, not a claim of fresh current-time endpoint readings.
- **Locations:** Koware’s New Legon, Accra setting; the Kuala Lumpur investigation setting; and historical-reference and Probe clocks are kept distinct. Individual deployed-member locations and conditions are not verified.
- **Status axes:** Process `DRAFTED / AWAITING_EDITORIAL_REVIEW`; editorial `UNREVIEWED`; persistence `DRAFTED_NOT_COMMITTED`; release `NOT_ELIGIBLE`; derivatives `NOT_COMMISSIONED`.
- **Framework status:** CC-01 and CC-02 v0.08.00 materials were read as proposed working candidates, not as evidence of owner ratification or deployed schema migration.

## B2 — Research/source register

- **Daily archive material:** E02.01 and E02.02 were retrieved as compiled source files; E02.03–E02.08 files and the existing prior episode-response compilation were retrieved. These contain multiple platform drafts and conflicting versions. Their presence does not establish human acceptance.
- **Episode reconciliation:** Eight archived installments cover ten working-day story dates because E02.08 covers 11–13 November. No extra source chronicles were invented.
- **Eternal Dreamfall:** The retrieved 250-entry list labels Week 96 as Septuagint continuation, covering Leviticus and Numbers. Its effective date-to-week anchor and daily passage assignment were not verified. The story uses the translation/transmission theme without claiming a dated assigned passage.
- **Verse:** The daily archive responses include different dated verses. This episode selects Philippians 4:8, cited in the 6 November source material, as the most relevant episode-wide verse. Provider/date attribution should be rechecked before publication; no other date-specific verse is presented as independently verified here.
- **Health & Safety:** The episode’s relevant topic is hazard identification and risk assessment, with clear communication and referral to the responsible local safety authority. Source responses refer to HS1000 Topic 3, “Proactive Safety: Mastering Hazard Identification & Risk Management,” but the course outline was not successfully retrieved in this review. The episode therefore does not claim that a named character completed that course or lesson.

The episode preserves reported-versus-confirmed distinctions and does not claim Koware was directly affected. These claims require source-level editorial fact-check before release.

# CLASSIES CHRONICLES
## Season 00 — Foundation Phase · Chapter 05 — *The Signal Before Sunrise*
### Episode 02 — *The Authority Behind the Arrow*

**Coverage:** Monday 2 – Friday 13 November 2026 (ten working days, eight chronicles; E02.08 covers 11–13 November)
**Status:** Provisional consolidated draft, awaiting human editorial review. Nothing has been committed or published.

---

## Cold Open: Four Languages, One Question

A building can print the same warning in four languages and still not say who was entitled to give it.

In Kuala Lumpur, four emergency notices hung near a lift in Malay, English, Mandarin and Tamil. Three pointed to one stairwell. The fourth pointed away. All four looked official, but looking official was not proof of anything.

The Classies field team had one job: observe, photograph and preserve. They were not to test a route or replace a safety instruction. Over ten days, Enka and the Koware team in New Legon would learn how much trouble a plausible document can cause.

---

## E02.01 — The Notice in Four Languages
**Monday, 2 November 2026**

Enka was awake before the compound stirred. His Eternal Dreamfall plan had reached Week 96 and the Septuagint, the long journey of meaning between languages. The weekly list named no passage for the day, so he left that line blank and carried the question with him: what survives translation, and what quietly changes?

In the Koware laboratory, three clocks sat on the console. The production clock read Accra, November 2026. The historical-reference clock read a July Sunday in 2003, Las Vegas. The Probe held its own separate reading. None of them said where the field team stood.

The analyst's photographs arrived from Kuala Lumpur. Three notices pointed to one stairwell, and one pointed elsewhere. The odd notice hung a little lower and was the only one with a printed date.

"Three copies can come from one mistake," the team lead said over the link.

The R&D Lab Specialist leaned in for a better look, and the chair under her slid away on a loose caster. She glided across the floor with a tray of printed photographs, and they fanned out behind her like a card trick. Enka caught the last one with two fingers and did not smile until she had stopped moving.

At the Monday briefing, Edliex listened through the status reports and then spoke.

"Three agreeing copies are one witness until someone proves they are three," Edliex said. "I'd record what the signs say and what the corridor is, and not let either stand in for the other."

Enka wrote **OBSERVED** on one page and **NOT YET VERIFIED** on the next. Under the second heading he listed the original instruction, its author, its approver and the translations' fidelity.

## E02.02 — The Cropped Truth
**Tuesday, 3 November 2026**

Enka's questions narrowed. Who requested the notice? Who wrote the wording? Who approved the translations, and when were the versions put up?

He called the Kuala Lumpur building manager. The video link froze mid-sentence on an expression of heroic concentration while Enka carried on talking to it for several seconds. He stopped when the Legal Journalist leaned into frame and said, "He can't hear you. He's a statue."

The link recovered. The manager explained that a notice passes through hands before it reaches a wall. One person asks, another words it, a third translates, and a fourth tapes it up. The finished sheet does not show that chain.

A team photograph arrived cropped. It showed the odd notice and the lift and nothing of the wall around them, and Enka asked for the uncropped original. He had learned to distrust a story that had been trimmed to fit. The manager promised to check the records.

The role check placed Health and Safety and personnel support in view. Enka took it as a reminder that a printed arrow moves people. It was not proof that any named person had done real work. The team stayed observation-only.

## E02.03 — The Unsigned Fourth Notice
**Wednesday, 4 November 2026**

The manager's reply came without the document Enka had asked for. It was a distribution register, and it showed that copies had been issued.

"Does it show who approved the instruction?" Enka asked.

"That the copies were tracked," the specialist said. "Not that the instruction was authorised."

Enka drew two columns: **WHAT THIS RECORD SHOWS** and **WHAT THIS RECORD DOES NOT SHOW**. Silence about a signature was not proof that no signature existed. It meant only that this record did not supply one.

He returned to the Health and Safety material on hazard identification. A disagreement between instructions was itself a warning sign, but that did not entitle a remote, unbriefed team to pick a route. Any assessment belonged to the building's responsible staff.

The register had to be printed for the evidence file, and the Koware printer chewed the first copy, shuddered and fed the page out in a spiral. It was stapled to the register's cover sheet. The specialist photographed it and filed it under **Evidence (Mechanical Opinion)**.

By evening the next request was specific: the original instruction, its approval record, its effective date and the issued versions. The fourth notice stayed unsigned.

## E02.04 — The Instruction No One Could Sign
**Thursday, 5 November 2026**

The Probe display changed in the middle of a review. The destination date flickered and settled on a new value. The lab lights dipped for half a second.

"Don't overwrite it," Enka said, already moving. "Save the raw response. Save every earlier reading."

The specialist caught the printout as it slid from the tray, then lunged for a thermos rolling off the bench. She came up tangled in a data cable with the thermos in one hand and the paper in the other, having saved both. The cable took longer.

The run record listed historical reference, current date, phase boundaries, oscillation index, amplitude and Probe value, each on its own line. The Probe recalculated against its inputs, so a changed date was not yet a changed destination. Enka's old design sheet, in his own handwriting, lay beside the console. So did the history that the cloned Edubeam had first been operated only by the Micro Computer Technology Institute of London. None of it replaced the readings.

He wrote three headings: **WHAT THE SOURCE RETURNED**, **WHAT THE CALCULATION MAY MEAN** and **WHAT WE HAVE NOT VERIFIED**. The Probe puzzle and the notice puzzle had to stay in separate files, so that neither could be used to explain the other away.

The team's supplied position was Kuala Lumpur. A Probe timestamp did not locate them in time.

## E02.05 — The Copies That Agreed
**Friday, 6 November 2026**

On Friday, the Director of Academic Programs, the Faculty Coordinator and the Quality Assurance and Compliance Manager lined up all four versions. Three agreed and one did not.

"Three agreeing copies aren't three independent witnesses," the Faculty Coordinator said. "They could all descend from one mistake."

At 10:40 Kuala Lumpur time, the building sounded an alarm. The field analyst's message arrived in a rush: a fire drill, announced only by the alarm itself. The team watched from the lobby and did not move.

Occupants poured from the lifts and split. Some followed the three matching notices and some followed the fourth. A knot of visitors stopped dead, looking from sign to sign. Then the building's wardens appeared in hi-vis vests with whistles and called people to one muster point by voice, cutting across the paper. The team recorded all of it and intervened in none.

"They used neither arrow," the analyst reported. "They used the wardens."

Enka read the footage twice. Whatever the wardens were following, it was not on the wall. The drill showed the danger of the disagreement but did not say which notice was right. The team agreed to preserve every version with its provenance, and the week closed with the original source still missing.

## E02.06 — The Line Beneath the Arrow
**Monday, 9 November 2026**

Four notices lay on the table in New Legon. Johnson Ortsin unfolded a site plan. "If three say east, we have a majority."

"A majority of paper," Derek Shiraska said. "Not a verified exit."

Matthias Agbenorku arrived with the operations notes. A work order marked **44-E** accounted for the notices being installed, but not for the authority behind them. The building manager had denied issuing them.

Edliex listened through the update and then spoke. "Don't argue about which arrow wins. Find out who had the right to draw it."

The team sent a narrow request to the responsible safety officer. A circled line in the Accra electricity-maintenance schedule raised equipment readiness. The schedule did not establish the supply status of Koware's own building, so Matthias asked for verification and did not declare an outage.

At 14:10, word came that the regional records office could trace Work Order 44-E to an older file transfer. A matching storage box had been found. Matthias and Johnson went at once, one on foot and one with the site plan under his arm. They made the office before closing, where a clerk showed them the shelf. The box was sealed, and neither of them touched it.

"We found the box," Johnson said.

"We found where the box is," Derek corrected, by phone.

## E02.07 — The Name the Paper Could Not Give
**Tuesday, 10 November 2026**

Mubarak Mohsen held the release request up to the office light. It had a transfer reference, a date and a handsome stamp. Beneath them was an empty line: **WHO IS AUTHORISED TO REQUEST RELEASE?**

The custodian would not move the box without a named, authorised requester. At the counter the air tightened. A queue formed behind Mubarak, someone checked a watch, and the stamp pad sat between him and the clerk like a dare.

On a screen in New Legon, Enka's draft message to Kuala Lumpur read: *We have found the authorising instruction. Proceed to check the route.*

"We have found a possible container for the instruction," Mubarak said. "The box is still sealed."

Enka deleted the sentence.

The revised request preserved the conflicting notices and asked the issuing office to establish its authority. It did not authorise a route inspection. Mubarak signed only for the request to be considered, never on behalf of the authority it needed. As he lifted the stamp, he planted it squarely on his own cuff, leaving a perfect official oval on his sleeve. The clerk looked at it and then at him. "That one," she said, "is valid."

A second question sat lower on the form: **DOES THE ORIGINAL INSTRUCTION STILL MATCH THE BUILDING AS IT STANDS TODAY?** Even a valid signature would not answer that.

## E02.08 — The Message That Stayed Unsent
**Wednesday 11 – Friday 13 November 2026**

**Wednesday.** The Legal Journalist's first act at the reception pinboard was to strike a word from a headline. **CONFIRMED** became **REPORTED**. The Press Coordinator read the draft. "It says the notices were approved and distributed."

"Distributed is what the work order suggests," the Marketing Manager said. "Approved is what we keep trying to prove."

Matthew 6:11, the day's Bible App reference, sat with Enka as he thought about reliable information for the next responsible step. The Legal Journalist reserved a blank rectangle labelled **AUTHORITY NOT YET VERIFIED**.

**Thursday.** The International Relations desk worked from three questions. What was the instruction's source? Who could authorise it? Who would check that it still suited the building? The Global Partnerships Registrar numbered the Kuala Lumpur papers. The Liaison logged each version's language, recipient and whether delivery was acknowledged. "An acknowledgement is not comprehension," the Liaison said. "And comprehension is not permission."

The Legal Journalist flagged a Ghanaian budget item for verification. It gave the Marketing desk something to study, but it was not a Koware contract. The team also watched reports of a communications outage and asked a practical question: if an approval request vanished into an inaccessible inbox, how would anyone prove what was sent? They proposed an offline decision record and a second contact channel. The registrar annotated the sealed-box line: *Location indicated; contents unknown; release authority unresolved.*

**Friday.** The pinboard was crowded. The Legal Journalist had sorted verified reports from developing ones: the Ghanaian budget item, the Ghana Stock Exchange discussion of blockchain and capital-market modernisation, the Ecobank JoyBusiness SME Fair and the Malaysian investigation into possible AI-chip re-exports. The Malaysia story shared a country with the Kuala Lumpur inquiry but none of its facts.

The Events and Tours Coordinator saw the SME fair as a chance for staff to observe local businesses. The Booking Agent kept a list of interested staff and a separate list of confirmed ones. "Those names mean they would like to attend," the Booking Agent said. "We don't have a confirmed booking."

Then the Booking Agent tried to slide an overloaded folder under the meeting-room door while balancing a stack of attendance forms. The folder wedged and the forms sprang up. One large sheet came down squarely on the Booking Agent's head like an oversized paper hat. The room forgot the paperwork for a moment.

Then it remembered. The Legal Journalist struck **CONFIRMED** from one more draft, because it was not.

The box still held a possible instruction. The release request still needed an authorised name. Even if the box opened, the document inside would have to be checked against the current building and the four language versions.

The message Enka had nearly sent said the team had an authoritative instruction. It would have implied more than the evidence showed, and it might have prompted action before anyone had verified a thing. It stayed unsent.

He looked at the blank authorisation line.

The notices had been distributed and the transfer record existed. The box had been found. No one had established who issued the instruction, whether it was authentic, or whether it still matched the building. The Classies team had not been authorised to test either route.

Enka closed the file. No one would turn a plausible document into a safety instruction because the story needed an ending.

The message stayed unsent. The investigation stayed open. For once, the blank line said exactly what it needed to say.

---

## Next Episode Preview: *E03*
The withheld message has consequences. A response arrives from the records office or a safety authority and settles one question while opening another. The sealed box may finally have a lawful route to being opened, or a custodian may refuse again. The Probe's changing readings get a separate technical review. In Kuala Lumpur, a warden who ignored the notices may know more than anyone expected. These are proposed directions only.

## The Classies Challenge: Episode 02

A facility displays four translated emergency notices. A distribution register shows they were circulated, and a storage record points to a sealed box that may hold the original instruction. The issuing authority has not been verified, and the building may have changed since the instruction was written.

**Before anyone can responsibly treat one of the displayed directions as the building's current emergency instruction, what must be established?**

- **A.** That at least three of the four notices agree with one another
- **B.** That the register shows the notices were circulated to the building
- **C.** That an authorised issuer approved the instruction, that it is the version in force, and that it still fits the building as it stands
- **D.** That the notice carries a printed date and an official-looking stamp
- **E.** That the field team has walked and timed both routes

| EVT-014 | Draft "authorising instruction found" message deleted before sending |
| **EVT-015 (new)** | Kuala Lumpur fire drill: wardens direct by voice; notices ignored; team observes only. **Editor-added; needs review** |

**Open threads:** original instruction; issuer identity; approval and effective date; translation fidelity; box release; current building fit; Probe interpretation; individual team positions; Koware reporting practice.
